<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Every closed smooth Riemannian surface admits a smooth isometric immersion into Euclidean four-space, without an orientability assumption. This resolves the closed-surface form of the classical four-dimensional isometric-immersion problem.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Every closed smooth Riemannian surface $(M,g)$ admits a $C^\infty$ isometric immersion into $(\mathbb{R}^4,\delta_4)$, where $\delta_4=\sum_{i=1}^4 dx_i^2$.
  - Explanation: Every closed smooth surface with a smooth Riemannian metric has a smooth isometric immersion into Euclidean four-space. Self-intersections are permitted, and orientability is not required.
- **C2** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-phase-data)): Let $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ be a smooth immersion with $B_F(p)\ne0$ for every $p$, and let $h_0$ be a smooth positive definite tensor. There are finitely many functions $\phi_\nu$ defined on coordinate patches, smooth cutoffs $\psi_\nu$ supported compactly in those patches, and smooth fiberwise linear forms $q_\nu$ on symmetric tensors, such that $$\label{eq:phase-decomposition} H=\sum_\nu b_\nu(H)^2\,d\phi_\nu^2, \qquad b_\nu(H)=\psi_\nu\sqrt{q_\nu(H)}$$ whenever $H$ is sufficiently close to $h_0$ in $C^0$. Every square root has a strict positive lower bound before multiplication by the cutoff. On the appropriate supports the differentials of $\phi_\nu$, $2\phi_\nu$, and $\phi_\nu\pm\phi_\mu$ for $\nu\ne\mu$ are nonzero and good. These conclusions persist for immersions in a sufficiently small $C^2$ neighborhood of $F$. Each phase or phase combination has a smooth complementary coordinate coframe on the support where it is used.
  - Explanation: A positive tensor near the reference metric decomposes into compactly supported positive primitive forms with admissible individual and combined phases.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-polynomial-phase-data)): Fix a metric $h_*$ and suppose that $f_z$, $0<z<z_0$, are smooth immersions satisfying uniform upper and positive lower metric bounds, $$\\|f_z\\|_{C^j}=O(z^{-j})\quad(0\le j\le3), \qquad \inf_{p\in M}\max_{\|Y\|=1}\|B_{f_z}(Y,Y)\|\ge c>0.$$ Then the phase data of Lemma <a href="#lem:phase-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phase-data">3</a>, with $h_0=h_*$, can be chosen on cells of diameter comparable to $z^6$. The number of cells and all fixed-order derivatives of their cutoff and phase data are polynomially bounded. The good-direction estimates and metric nondegeneracy persist throughout the $C^2$ ball of radius $z^4$ about $f_z$, for all sufficiently small $z$.* *No regularity with respect to $z$ is required. The construction uses a bounded number of phase weights, independent of the number of cells. The inverse coframes and the reciprocals of the relevant second-form lengths have polynomial bounds. Consequently every fixed finite differential construction formed from these data by sums, products, derivatives, metric inversion, normal projection, and division by the stated nonzero quantities has polynomial bounds, provided its input has the required finite polynomial derivative bounds. Its exponents depend only on the derivative orders and on the input exponents, not on their implicit constants.
  - Explanation: Uniform metric and second-form margins allow phase data on shrinking cells with polynomial bounds at each fixed derivative order.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-primitive-addition)): Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface and $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ a smooth immersion whose second fundamental form is nonzero as a tensor at every point. Assume $F$ has a preferred normal $n$. Let $D$ be a disk with smooth boundary $C_0$ and coordinates $(x,y)$ on a neighborhood of $\overline D$. Let $a\in C^\infty(M)$ be nonnegative, positive exactly on $D$, and put $Y_0=\partial_y$. Suppose $$\operatorname{Hess}_{\gamma(F)}x(\partial_y,\partial_y)>0 \quad\text{on }\overline D. \label{eq:primitive-hessian}$$ Let $C_1,\ldots,C_m$ be finitely many later curves with nonzero smooth vector fields $Y_i$ on their neighborhoods. Assume the following:* 1. *All curves $C_0,\ldots,C_m$ are pairwise transverse and have no triple intersection.* 2. *On the portion of each later curve $C_i$, $1\le i\le m$, in $\overline D$, the vector $\partial_y$ is tangent to $C_i$ at only finitely many points.* 3. *At every $C_i\cap C_l\cap D$ with distinct $i,l>0$, $dx(Y_i)\ne0$ and $dx(Y_l)\ne0$.* 4. *Conditions <a href="#eq:boundary-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-invariants">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> hold for all indices $0,\ldots,m$.* *Then, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there are a smooth immersion $F_+:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ and a preferred normal $n_+$ such that $$\gamma(F_+)=\gamma_+:=\gamma(F)+a^2\,dx^2, \qquad B_{F_+}\ne0\ \text{at every point},$$ conditions <a href="#eq:boundary-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-invariants">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> hold for $(F_+,n_+)$ and all later indices $1,\ldots,m$, and $$\\|F_+-F\\|_{C^2(M\setminus D)}<\varepsilon.$$ The tensor $a^2dx^2$ is extended by zero outside the coordinate neighborhood.
  - Explanation: A positive primitive metric can be added exactly while preserving the normal and crossing conditions needed by later disks and controlling the exterior change.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-small-increment)): Let the phase data above be fixed. Let $P_1,\ldots,P_d$ be fixed smooth tensor-valued differential operators polynomial above order two, and put $$\mathcal P(G)=\gamma(G)+\sum_{i=1}^d\epsilon^i P_i(G), \qquad 0\leq\epsilon\leq1.$$ There is a finite exponent $p$, depending on these operators and independent of the integer $q$ below, with the following property. Suppose $G$ is an immersion whose 2-jets satisfy the stated compact margins, $H\in\mathcal U_0$, and $$j^2G=O_s(1),\qquad H=O_s(1).$$ For every positive integer $q$, every $\delta>0$, and scales $0<\tau\leq s\leq1$, define $$\eta=\frac\tau s+\epsilon\tau^{-p}.$$ If $\eta$ and $\delta/\tau$ are sufficiently small, there is a smooth displacement $U$ such that $$\label{eq:small-increment-estimate} U=O_\tau(\delta\tau),\qquad \mathcal P(G+U)-\mathcal P(G)-\delta^2H =O_\tau\left(\delta\eta^q+\frac{\delta^3}{\tau}\right).$$ The smallness thresholds and every fixed output norm use only finitely many weighted input bounds. Their required orders may increase with $q$. Each local summand of $U$ is supported in an original phase support or an intersection of two such supports. If the perturbation sum is absent, take $\eta=\tau/s$.
  - Explanation: Finite oscillatory corrections add a positive tensor to prescribed finite accuracy, with controlled displacement and remainder for a polynomial differential perturbation.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-exact-correction)): Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface, and let $h_*$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on $M$. Fix background norms. There are positive integers $R,N$ with the following property. Suppose that $f_z:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ are smooth immersions for sufficiently small $z>0$, and that $$\begin{aligned} \\|f_z\\|_{C^m}&=O(z^{-m}) &&(0\le m\le R+2),\label{eq:exact-input-jets}\\ \\|\gamma(f_z)-h_*\\|_{C^R}&=O(z^N),\label{eq:exact-input-metric}\\ \min_{p\in M}\max_{\|u\|=1}\|B_{f_z}(u,u)\|&\ge c>0. \label{eq:exact-input-second-form} \end{aligned}$$ Then, for all sufficiently small $z$, there is a smooth immersion $\widetilde f_z:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ such that $$\gamma(\widetilde f_z)=h_*,\qquad \\|\widetilde f_z-f_z\\|_{C^2}=o(1).$$ The integers $R,N$ do not depend on the constants in <a href="#eq:exact-input-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-input-jets">[eq:exact-input-jets]</a>–<a href="#eq:exact-input-second-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-input-second-form">[eq:exact-input-second-form]</a>. The threshold on $z$ may depend on them. No regularity of the family with respect to $z$ is required or asserted.
  - Explanation: Sufficient finite-order metric accuracy and polynomial jet bounds permit an exact smooth correction that tends to the approximate immersion in the second derivative norm.
- **C7** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-order-smoothing)): For each positive integer $r$, there are linear smoothing operators $S_s$, $0<s\le s_0$, on smooth sections of any fixed smooth vector bundle over $M$, with the following properties. For fixed nonnegative integers $q,m$, and $0\le j\le r$, $$\begin{aligned} \\|S_s u\\|_{C^{q+m}}&\le C_{q,m,r}s^{-m}\\|u\\|_{C^q}, \label{eq:smooth-derivative}\\ \\|u-S_su\\|_{C^{q+j}}&\le C_{q,j,r}s^{r-j}\\|u\\|_{C^{q+r}}, \label{eq:smooth-approximation}\\ \\|S_su\\|_{C^m}&\le C_{m,r}\\|u\\|_{C^m}. \label{eq:smooth-boundedness} \end{aligned}$$ The constants use only the fixed atlas, bundle, and displayed orders.
  - Explanation: Linear smoothing on fixed bundles controls derivative growth and approximation tails using finitely many vanishing moments.
- **C8** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-exact-step)): Under <a href="#eq:exact-step-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-step-hypotheses">[eq:exact-step-hypotheses]</a>, take either $\delta'=\tau^k$ or $\delta'=\tau^{k+1}$. For sufficiently small $t$, there is a smooth increment $U$ such that, with $$G'=G+U,\qquad H'=\delta'^{-2}(h_*-\gamma(G')), \qquad \mathcal S'_m=\mathcal S_m(\tau;G',H'),$$ for every fixed $m\ge0$, $$\begin{aligned} \|U\|_{m,\tau}&\le C_m(z,k)\delta\tau, \label{eq:exact-increment-bound}\\ \mathcal S'_m &\le \\|G\\|_{C^2}+\\|h_*\\|_{C^0} +C_m(z,k)t^{1/5}(1+\mathcal S_m). \label{eq:exact-all-order-recurrence} \end{aligned}$$ The scale threshold and the constants are independent of derivatives of the true inputs $G,H$ above the controlled orders $r(k)+2,r(k)$. For fixed $k$, the inverse threshold is polynomially bounded in $z^{-1}$; for fixed $k,m$, the constants have the same property.
  - Explanation: An increment built from smoothed inputs updates the unsmoothed immersion and damps every fixed weighted derivative norm through a recurrence.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-velocity-plane)): On a neighborhood of $\overline D$ and a sufficiently small neighborhood of the 2-jet section of $F$, the vectors $Y,C$ are independent, $P$ is a smooth 2-plane, and $R_0$ is bounded below by a positive constant. Projection to $P$ restricts to an isomorphism on the normal plane of $G$. There is a smooth orthonormal frame $(e_1,e_2)$ of $P$, depending smoothly on these jets, such that $e_1=v/\|v\|$ on a collar of $C_0$.
  - Explanation: The transverse Hessian condition and boundary normal data supply an independent transverse derivative pair, a smooth perpendicular plane, and a compatible local frame.
- **C10** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-loop-density)): Let a smooth family of circle-valued paths $p_b(t)\in\mathbb{R}^2$ and targets $c_b\in\mathbb{R}^2$ be parametrized by a finite-dimensional parameter $b$. On a compact parameter set, suppose each $c_b$ lies in the interior of the convex hull of $p_b$. Then, on a neighborhood of that set, there are smoothly varying strictly positive densities $\rho_b(t)$ such that $$\operatorname{avg}_t\rho_b=1,\qquad \operatorname{avg}_t(\rho_b p_b)=c_b.$$ These densities may be patched with any already prescribed positive solutions on an overlapping parameter neighborhood.
  - Explanation: Strict interior convex-hull membership permits positive smooth densities with the prescribed mass and vector average.
- **C11** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-small-value-derivative)): Let $K$ be a compact subset of a finite union of smooth embedded curves in the interior of a coordinate disk. Suppose the curves have only finitely many intersections and only finitely many tangencies to $\partial_y$ near $K$, with no shared curve arcs. Given $B>0$ and $\epsilon>0$, there is $h\in C_c^\infty(D)$ such that $$\\|h\\|_{C^0}<\epsilon,\qquad h_y=B\quad\text{on }K.$$ The support may be kept in any fixed precompact open neighborhood of $K$.
  - Explanation: Finite intersections and transverse tangencies permit a compactly supported function whose value is small and whose transverse derivative is prescribed along the selected curve set.
- **C12** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-velocity-loop)): Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:primitive-addition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:primitive-addition">5</a>, fix any finite threshold $T$. There is a neighborhood of the 2-jet section of $F$ over $\overline D$ and a small exterior neighborhood, on which one can choose smooth $2\pi$-periodic vectors $V,w$ as in <a href="#eq:velocity-plane" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:velocity-plane">[eq:velocity-plane]</a>–<a href="#eq:leading-velocity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leading-velocity">[eq:leading-velocity]</a>, with $$\|V\|=R_0>0,\qquad \operatorname{avg}V=v,\qquad \operatorname{avg}w=0.$$ They depend smoothly on position and the 2-jet; $w$ extends smoothly by zero outside $D$. The leading derivatives $(X,Y)$ have metric $\gamma(G)+a^2dx^2$. At $G=F$, the limiting normal data defined in <a href="#eq:limiting-mixed-components" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-mixed-components">[eq:limiting-mixed-components]</a> have uniform bounds <a href="#eq:derivative-independent-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:derivative-independent-bounds">[eq:derivative-independent-bounds]</a>, a positive lower bound for $S_0$, and the collar inequality <a href="#eq:collar-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-inequality">[eq:collar-inequality]</a>. On the remaining compact portions of the later curves, the only zeros of $\alpha_\theta$ are the two turns, and there $\|N_0^*\|>T$. The bounds for $S_0,1/S_0,D_0^*$ and the error $E_0$ can be fixed before choosing $T$ and $B_*$.
  - Explanation: A smooth periodic loop realizes the leading metric and prescribed average, vanishes smoothly outside the disk, and supplies large transverse normal components at the turns.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-primitive-realization)): Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface and let $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ be a smooth immersion whose second fundamental form is nonzero at every point. Let $D$ be a disk with coordinates $x,y$ on a neighborhood of its closure, and let $a$ be a smooth nonnegative function, positive on $D$ and zero on its exterior. Suppose the following data are defined smoothly on a neighborhood of the $2$-jets of $F$ over $\overline D$ and a collar. For a map $G$ with jets in that neighborhood, set $$Y=G_y,\qquad C=G_{yy},\qquad P=\operatorname{span}(Y,C)^\perp,\qquad v=PG_x,\qquad R_0=(\|v\|^2+a^2)^{1/2}.$$ Assume that $Y,C$ are independent and $R_0>0$. Suppose there is a smooth $2\pi$-periodic vector $V=V(G;\theta)$, depending only on position and the $2$-jet of $G$, such that $$V\in P,\qquad \|V\|=R_0,\qquad \operatorname{avg}_\theta V=v.$$ Assume also that $V-v$ extends smoothly by zero across the boundary of $D$. These hypotheses hold for the loop of Lemma <a href="#lem:velocity-loop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:velocity-loop">15</a>.* *Put $h_*=\gamma(F)+a^2dx^2$, extending the added tensor by zero. For all sufficiently small $z>0$ there is a smooth immersion $\widetilde f_z:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ with $\gamma(\widetilde f_z)=h_*$. To state its derivatives, evaluate the preceding quantities at $G=F$ and write $X=F_x-v+V$. Uniformly on $\overline D$, with periodic arguments evaluated at $\theta=x/z$, $$\begin{aligned} (\widetilde f_z)_x&=X+o(1),& (\widetilde f_z)_y&=Y+o(1),\\ (\widetilde f_z)_{yy}&=C+o(1),& (\widetilde f_z)_{xy}&=X_y+o(1),\\ z(\widetilde f_z)_{xx}&=V_\theta+o(1). \end{aligned} \label{eq:realization-asymptotics}$$ Here $X_y$ means differentiation with $\theta$ held fixed. On the exterior of $D$, $\widetilde f_z$ converges to $F$ in $C^2$. There is a constant $c>0$, independent of sufficiently small $z$, such that $$\min_{p\in M}\max_{\|u\|=1} \|B_{\widetilde f_z}(u,u)\|\ge c,$$ where the unit vectors use the fixed background tangent norm.
  - Explanation: The velocity-loop data can be realized by exact smooth immersions with specified derivative asymptotics and a uniform nonzero second-form margin.
- **C14** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-periodic-expansion)): Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:primitive-realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:primitive-realization">16</a>, for each integer $L\ge2$ there are periodic differential operators $U_1,\ldots,U_L$ with values in $\mathbb{R}^4$, and symmetric tensor differential operators $E_1,\ldots,E_{L-1}$, for which $$f_z[G]=G+\sum_{j=1}^L z^jU_j(G;x/z) \label{eq:finite-ansatz}$$ has the exact expansion $$\gamma(f_z[G]) =a^2dx^2+\mathcal P_z(G) +\sum_{r=L}^{2L}z^r\mathcal R_r(G;x/z), \qquad \mathcal P_z(G)=\gamma(G)+\sum_{r=1}^{L-1}z^rE_r(G). \label{eq:finite-metric-expansion}$$ Each $U_j$ has mean zero. The operators $U_j,E_r,\mathcal R_r$ are smooth, supported on $\overline D$, and polynomial in jets above order two, with smooth coefficients in the $2$-jet and position, and, where appropriate, $\theta$. One may take the differential orders of $U_j$ and $E_r$ to be at most $2j$ and $2r+2$, respectively.
  - Explanation: A finite recursion removes periodic metric fluctuations to the selected order, leaving mean differential operators and a finite remainder.
- **C15** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-spherical-preparation)): Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface and let $I:M\to S^3$ be a smooth immersion. There are constants $C,c>0$ and $\varepsilon_0>0$ with the following property. For every finite set $P\subset M$ and every $0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_0$, there is a smooth immersion $I_P:M\to S^3$ such that $$B_{I_P}^{S^3}(p)=0\quad(p\in P),\qquad \\|I_P-I\\|_{C^1}<\varepsilon,\qquad \\|I_P\\|_{C^2}\le C, \qquad I_P^*\delta_4\ge c\,g_{\mathrm{bg}},$$ where $g_{\mathrm{bg}}$ is the fixed background metric. The constants are independent of the number and separation of the points of $P$.
  - Explanation: A spherical immersion can be changed near any finite set so that its spherical second form vanishes there, with uniform low-order bounds.
- **C16** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-global-preparation)): Let $(M,g)$ be a closed smooth Riemannian surface. There exist a smooth immersion $F^0:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$, a smooth unit normal $n^0$, and a finite list $$(D_j,x_j,y_j,a_j),\qquad 1\le j\le K,$$ with the following properties. Each $D_j$ is a coordinate disk with smooth boundary $C_j$; $(x_j,y_j)$ are coordinates on a neighborhood of $\overline D_j$; and $a_j$ is smooth, positive on $D_j$, and zero on its exterior. Set $Y_j=\partial_{y_j}$ and $$\gamma^j=\gamma(F^0)+\sum_{i=1}^j a_i^2(dx_i)^2, \qquad \gamma^0=\gamma(F^0).$$ Then:* 1. *$\gamma^K=g$, and $\operatorname{Hess}_{\gamma^{j-1}}x_j(Y_j,Y_j)>0$ on $\overline D_j$.* 2. *The boundaries are pairwise transverse and have no triple intersection. For $i\ne j$, $Y_j$ is tangent to $C_i$ at only finitely many points of $\overline D_j$. At $C_i\cap C_l\cap D_j$, with $i,l,j$ distinct, both $dx_j(Y_i)$ and $dx_j(Y_l)$ are nonzero.* 3. *$B_{F^0}$ is nowhere the zero tensor, and $F^0,n^0$ satisfy all boundary conditions <a href="#eq:boundary-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-invariants">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> for this list.
  - Explanation: A spherical starting immersion and an ordered finite primitive list give the target metric while arranging phase Hessians and all boundary invariants.

      "kind": "bound",
      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-polynomial-phase-data",
          "exact_quote": "Fix a metric $h_*$ and suppose that $f_z$, $0<z<z_0$, are smooth immersions satisfying uniform upper and positive lower metric bounds, $$\\|f_z\\|_{C^j}=O(z^{-j})\\quad(0\\le j\\le3),\n \\qquad\n \\inf_{p\\in M}\\max_{|Y|=1}|B_{f_z}(Y,Y)|\\ge c>0.$$ Then the phase data of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:phase-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:phase-data\">3</a>, with $h_0=h_*$, can be chosen on cells of diameter comparable to $z^6$. The number of cells and all fixed-order derivatives of their cutoff and phase data are polynomially bounded. The good-direction estimates and metric nondegeneracy persist throughout the $C^2$ ball of radius $z^4$ about $f_z$, for all sufficiently small $z$.*\n\n*No regularity with respect to $z$ is required. The construction uses a bounded number of phase weights, independent of the number of cells. The inverse coframes and the reciprocals of the relevant second-form lengths have polynomial bounds. Consequently every fixed finite differential construction formed from these data by sums, products, derivatives, metric inversion, normal projection, and division by the stated nonzero quantities has polynomial bounds, provided its input has the required finite polynomial derivative bounds. Its exponents depend only on the derivative orders and on the input exponents, not on their implicit constants.",
          "source_id": "PM-333.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-lem-polynomial-phase-data"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-primitive-addition",
        "exact_quote": "Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface and $F:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ a smooth immersion whose second fundamental form is nonzero as a tensor at every point. Assume $F$ has a preferred normal $n$. Let $D$ be a disk with smooth boundary $C_0$ and coordinates $(x,y)$ on a neighborhood of $\\overline D$. Let $a\\in C^\\infty(M)$ be nonnegative, positive exactly on $D$, and put $Y_0=\\partial_y$. Suppose $$\\operatorname{Hess}_{\\gamma(F)}x(\\partial_y,\\partial_y)>0\n \\quad\\text{on }\\overline D.\n \\label{eq:primitive-hessian}$$ Let $C_1,\\ldots,C_m$ be finitely many later curves with nonzero smooth vector fields $Y_i$ on their neighborhoods. Assume the following:*\n\n1.  *All curves $C_0,\\ldots,C_m$ are pairwise transverse and have no triple intersection.*\n\n2.  *On the portion of each later curve $C_i$, $1\\le i\\le m$, in $\\overline D$, the vector $\\partial_y$ is tangent to $C_i$ at only finitely many points.*\n\n3.  *At every $C_i\\cap C_l\\cap D$ with distinct $i,l>0$, $dx(Y_i)\\ne0$ and $dx(Y_l)\\ne0$.*\n\n4.  *Conditions <a href=\"#eq:boundary-invariants\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:boundary-invariants\">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> hold for all indices $0,\\ldots,m$.*\n\n*Then, for every $\\varepsilon>0$, there are a smooth immersion $F_+:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ and a preferred normal $n_+$ such that $$\\gamma(F_+)=\\gamma_+:=\\gamma(F)+a^2\\,dx^2,\n \\qquad B_{F_+}\\ne0\\ \\text{at every point},$$ conditions <a href=\"#eq:boundary-invariants\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:boundary-invariants\">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> hold for $(F_+,n_+)$ and all later indices $1,\\ldots,m$, and $$\\|F_+-F\\|_{C^2(M\\setminus D)}<\\varepsilon.$$ The tensor $a^2dx^2$ is extended by zero outside the coordinate neighborhood.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-small-increment",
        "exact_quote": "Let the phase data above be fixed. Let $P_1,\\ldots,P_d$ be fixed smooth tensor-valued differential operators polynomial above order two, and put $$\\mathcal P(G)=\\gamma(G)+\\sum_{i=1}^d\\epsilon^i P_i(G),\n \\qquad 0\\leq\\epsilon\\leq1.$$ There is a finite exponent $p$, depending on these operators and independent of the integer $q$ below, with the following property. Suppose $G$ is an immersion whose 2-jets satisfy the stated compact margins, $H\\in\\mathcal U_0$, and $$j^2G=O_s(1),\\qquad H=O_s(1).$$ For every positive integer $q$, every $\\delta>0$, and scales $0<\\tau\\leq s\\leq1$, define $$\\eta=\\frac\\tau s+\\epsilon\\tau^{-p}.$$ If $\\eta$ and $\\delta/\\tau$ are sufficiently small, there is a smooth displacement $U$ such that $$\\label{eq:small-increment-estimate}\n U=O_\\tau(\\delta\\tau),\\qquad\n \\mathcal P(G+U)-\\mathcal P(G)-\\delta^2H\n   =O_\\tau\\left(\\delta\\eta^q+\\frac{\\delta^3}{\\tau}\\right).$$ The smallness thresholds and every fixed output norm use only finitely many weighted input bounds. Their required orders may increase with $q$. Each local summand of $U$ is supported in an original phase support or an intersection of two such supports. If the perturbation sum is absent, take $\\eta=\\tau/s$.",

      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-exact-correction",
        "exact_quote": "Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface, and let $h_*$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on $M$. Fix background norms. There are positive integers $R,N$ with the following property. Suppose that $f_z:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ are smooth immersions for sufficiently small $z>0$, and that $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|f_z\\|_{C^m}&=O(z^{-m}) &&(0\\le m\\le R+2),\\label{eq:exact-input-jets}\\\\\n \\|\\gamma(f_z)-h_*\\|_{C^R}&=O(z^N),\\label{eq:exact-input-metric}\\\\\n \\min_{p\\in M}\\max_{|u|=1}|B_{f_z}(u,u)|&\\ge c>0.\n \\label{eq:exact-input-second-form}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then, for all sufficiently small $z$, there is a smooth immersion $\\widetilde f_z:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ such that $$\\gamma(\\widetilde f_z)=h_*,\\qquad\n \\|\\widetilde f_z-f_z\\|_{C^2}=o(1).$$ The integers $R,N$ do not depend on the constants in <a href=\"#eq:exact-input-jets\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:exact-input-jets\">[eq:exact-input-jets]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:exact-input-second-form\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:exact-input-second-form\">[eq:exact-input-second-form]</a>. The threshold on $z$ may depend on them. No regularity of the family with respect to $z$ is required or asserted.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-exact-correction",
          "exact_quote": "Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface, and let $h_*$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on $M$. Fix background norms. There are positive integers $R,N$ with the following property. Suppose that $f_z:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ are smooth immersions for sufficiently small $z>0$, and that $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|f_z\\|_{C^m}&=O(z^{-m}) &&(0\\le m\\le R+2),\\label{eq:exact-input-jets}\\\\\n \\|\\gamma(f_z)-h_*\\|_{C^R}&=O(z^N),\\label{eq:exact-input-metric}\\\\\n \\min_{p\\in M}\\max_{|u|=1}|B_{f_z}(u,u)|&\\ge c>0.\n \\label{eq:exact-input-second-form}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then, for all sufficiently small $z$, there is a smooth immersion $\\widetilde f_z:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ such that $$\\gamma(\\widetilde f_z)=h_*,\\qquad\n \\|\\widetilde f_z-f_z\\|_{C^2}=o(1).$$ The integers $R,N$ do not depend on the constants in <a href=\"#eq:exact-input-jets\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:exact-input-jets\">[eq:exact-input-jets]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:exact-input-second-form\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:exact-input-second-form\">[eq:exact-input-second-form]</a>.           "anchor": "stmt-prop-exact-correction",
          "exact_quote": "Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface, and let $h_*$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on $M$. Fix background norms. There are positive integers $R,N$ with the following property. Suppose that $f_z:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ are smooth immersions for sufficiently small $z>0$, and that $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|f_z\\|_{C^m}&=O(z^{-m}) &&(0\\le m\\le R+2),\\label{eq:exact-input-jets}\\\\\n \\|\\gamma(f_z)-h_*\\|_{C^R}&=O(z^N),\\label{eq:exact-input-metric}\\\\\n \\min_{p\\in M}\\max_{|u|=1}|B_{f_z}(u,u)|&\\ge c>0.\n \\label{eq:exact-input-second-form}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then, for all sufficiently small $z$, there is a smooth immersion $\\widetilde f_z:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ such that $$\\gamma(\\widetilde f_z)=h_*,\\qquad\n \\|\\widetilde f_z-f_z\\|_{C^2}=o(1).$$ The integers $R,N$ do not depend on the constants in <a href=\"#eq:exact-input-jets\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:exact-input-jets\">[eq:exact-input-jets]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:exact-input-second-form\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:exact-input-second-form\">[eq:exact-input-second-form]</a>. The threshold on $z$ may depend on them. No regularity of the family with respect to $z$ is required or asserted.",
          "source_id": "PM-333.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-prop-exact-correction"
    },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-order-smoothing",
        "exact_quote": "For each positive integer $r$, there are linear smoothing operators $S_s$, $0<s\\le s_0$, on smooth sections of any fixed smooth vector bundle over $M$, with the following properties. For fixed nonnegative integers $q,m$, and $0\\le j\\le r$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|S_s u\\|_{C^{q+m}}&\\le C_{q,m,r}s^{-m}\\|u\\|_{C^q},\n \\label{eq:smooth-derivative}\\\\\n \\|u-S_su\\|_{C^{q+j}}&\\le C_{q,j,r}s^{r-j}\\|u\\|_{C^{q+r}},\n \\label{eq:smooth-approximation}\\\\\n \\|S_su\\|_{C^m}&\\le C_{m,r}\\|u\\|_{C^m}.\n \\label{eq:smooth-boundedness}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The constants use only the fixed atlas, bundle, and displayed orders.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-velocity-plane",
        "exact_quote": "On a neighborhood of $\\overline D$ and a sufficiently small neighborhood of the 2-jet section of $F$, the vectors $Y,C$ are independent, $P$ is a smooth 2-plane, and $R_0$ is bounded below by a positive constant. Projection to $P$ restricts to an isomorphism on the normal plane of $G$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-loop-density",
        "exact_quote": "Let a smooth family of circle-valued paths $p_b(t)\\in\\mathbb{R}^2$ and targets $c_b\\in\\mathbb{R}^2$ be parametrized by a finite-dimensional parameter $b$. On a compact parameter set, suppose each $c_b$ lies in the interior of the convex hull of $p_b$. Then, on a neighborhood of that set, there are smoothly varying strictly positive densities $\\rho_b(t)$ such that $$\\operatorname{avg}_t\\rho_b=1,\\qquad \\operatorname{avg}_t(\\rho_b p_b)=c_b.$$ These densities may be patched with any already prescribed positive solutions on an overlapping parameter neighborhood.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-small-value-derivative",
        "exact_quote": "Let $K$ be a compact subset of a finite union of smooth embedded curves in the interior of a coordinate disk. Suppose the curves have only finitely many intersections and only finitely many tangencies to $\\partial_y$ near $K$, with no shared curve arcs. Given $B>0$ and $\\epsilon>0$, there is $h\\in C_c^\\infty(D)$ such that $$\\|h\\|_{C^0}<\\epsilon,\\qquad h_y=B\\quad\\text{on }K.$$ The support may be kept in any fixed precompact open neighborhood of $K$.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-velocity-loop",
        "exact_quote": "Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:primitive-addition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:primitive-addition\">5</a>, fix any finite threshold $T$. There is a neighborhood of the 2-jet section of $F$ over $\\overline D$ and a small exterior neighborhood, on which one can choose smooth $2\\pi$-periodic vectors $V,w$ as in <a href=\"#eq:velocity-plane\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:velocity-plane\">[eq:velocity-plane]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:leading-velocity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:leading-velocity\">[eq:leading-velocity]</a>, with $$|V|=R_0>0,\\qquad \\operatorname{avg}V=v,\\qquad \\operatorname{avg}w=0.$$ They depend smoothly on position and the 2-jet; $w$ extends smoothly by zero outside $D$. The leading derivatives $(X,Y)$ have metric $\\gamma(G)+a^2dx^2$. At $G=F$, the limiting normal data defined in <a href=\"#eq:limiting-mixed-components\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:limiting-mixed-components\">[eq:limiting-mixed-components]</a> have uniform bounds <a href=\"#eq:derivative-independent-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:derivative-independent-bounds\">[eq:derivative-independent-bounds]</a>, a positive lower bound for $S_0$, and the collar inequality <a href=\"#eq:collar-inequality\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:collar-inequality\">[eq:collar-inequality]</a>. On the remaining compact portions of the later curves, the only zeros of $\\alpha_\\theta$ are the two turns, and there $|N_0^*|>T$. The bounds for $S_0,1/S_0,D_0^*$ and the error $E_0$ can be fixed before choosing $T$ and $B_*$.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-velocity-loop",
          "exact_quote": "Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:primitive-addition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:primitive-addition\">5</a>, fix any finite threshold $T$. There is a neighborhood of the 2-jet section of $F$ over $\\overline D$ and a small exterior neighborhood, on which one can choose smooth $2\\pi$-periodic vectors $V,w$ as in <a href=\"#eq:velocity-plane\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:velocity-plane\">[eq:velocity-plane]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:leading-velocity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:leading-velocity\">[eq:leading-velocity]</a>, with $$|V|=R_0>0,\\qquad \\operatorname{avg}V=v,\\qquad \\operatorname{avg}w=0.$$ They depend smoothly on position and the 2-jet; $w$ extends smoothly by zero outside $D$. The leading derivatives $(X,Y)$ have metric $\\gamma(G)+a^2dx^2$. At $G=F$, the limiting normal data defined in <a href=\"#eq:limiting-mixed-components\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:limiting-mixed-components\">[eq:limiting-mixed-components]</a> have uniform bounds <a href=\"#eq:derivative-independent-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:derivative-independent-bounds\">[eq:derivative-independent-bounds]</a>, a positive lower bound for $S_0$, and the collar inequality <a href=\"#eq:collar-inequality\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:collar-inequality\">[eq:collar-inequality]</a>. On the remaining compact portions of the later curves, the only zeros of $\\alpha_\\theta$ are the two turns, and there $|N_0^*|>T$. The bounds for $S_0,1/S_0,D_0^*$ and the error $E_0$ can be fixed before choosing $T$ and $B_*$.",
          "source_id": "PM-333.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:primitive-addition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:primitive-addition\">5</a>, fix any finite threshold $T$. The leading derivatives $(X,Y)$ have metric $\\gamma(G)+a^2dx^2$. At $G=F$, the limiting normal data defined in <a href=\"#eq:limiting-mixed-components\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:limiting-mixed-components\">[eq:limiting-mixed-components]</a> have uniform bounds <a href=\"#eq:derivative-independent-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:derivative-independent-bounds\">[eq:derivative-independent-bounds]</a>, a positive lower bound for $S_0$, and the collar inequality <a href=\"#eq:collar-inequality\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:collar-inequality\">[eq:collar-inequality]</a>. On the remaining compact portions of the later curves, the only zeros of $\\alpha_\\theta$ are the two turns, and there $|N_0^*|>T$. The bounds for $S_0,1/S_0,D_0^*$ and the error $E_0$ can be fixed before choosing $T$ and $B_*$.",
      "explanation": "A smooth periodic loop realizes the leading metric and prescribed average, vanishes smoothly outside the disk, and supplies large transverse normal components at the turns.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-primitive-realization",
        "exact_quote": "Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface and let $F:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ be a smooth immersion whose second fundamental form is nonzero at every point. Let $D$ be a disk with coordinates $x,y$ on a neighborhood of its closure, and let $a$ be a smooth nonnegative function, positive on $D$ and zero on its exterior. Suppose the following data are defined smoothly on a neighborhood of the $2$-jets of $F$ over $\\overline D$ and a collar. For a map $G$ with jets in that neighborhood, set $$Y=G_y,\\qquad C=G_{yy},\\qquad\n P=\\operatorname{span}(Y,C)^\\perp,\\qquad\n v=PG_x,\\qquad R_0=(|v|^2+a^2)^{1/2}.$$ Assume that $Y,C$ are independent and $R_0>0$. Suppose there is a smooth $2\\pi$-periodic vector $V=V(G;\\theta)$, depending only on position and the $2$-jet of $G$, such that $$V\\in P,\\qquad |V|=R_0,\\qquad \\operatorname{avg}_\\theta V=v.$$ Assume also that $V-v$ extends smoothly by zero across the boundary of $D$. These hypotheses hold for the loop of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:velocity-loop\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:velocity-loop\">15</a>.*\n\n\n*Put $h_*=\\gamma(F)+a^2dx^2$, extending the added tensor by zero. For all sufficiently small $z>0$ there is a smooth immersion $\\widetilde f_z:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$ with $\\gamma(\\widetilde f_z)=h_*$. To state its derivatives, evaluate the preceding quantities at $G=F$ and write $X=F_x-v+V$. Uniformly on $\\overline D$, with periodic arguments evaluated at $\\theta=x/z$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n (\\widetilde f_z)_x&=X+o(1),&\n (\\widetilde f_z)_y&=Y+o(1),\\\\\n (\\widetilde f_z)_{yy}&=C+o(1),&\n (\\widetilde f_z)_{xy}&=X_y+o(1),\\\\\n z(\\widetilde f_z)_{xx}&=V_\\theta+o(1).\n\\end{aligned}\n\\label{eq:realization-asymptotics}$$ Here $X_y$ means differentiation with $\\theta$ held fixed. On the exterior of $D$, $\\widetilde f_z$ converges to $F$ in $C^2$. There is a constant $c>0$, independent of sufficiently small $z$, such that $$\\min_{p\\in M}\\max_{|u|=1}\n       |B_{\\widetilde f_z}(u,u)|\\ge c,$$ where the unit vectors use the fixed background tangent norm.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-periodic-expansion",
        "exact_quote": "Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:primitive-realization\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:primitive-realization\">16</a>, for each integer $L\\ge2$ there are periodic differential operators $U_1,\\ldots,U_L$ with values in $\\mathbb{R}^4$, and symmetric tensor differential operators $E_1,\\ldots,E_{L-1}$, for which $$f_z[G]=G+\\sum_{j=1}^L z^jU_j(G;x/z)\n \\label{eq:finite-ansatz}$$ has the exact expansion $$\\gamma(f_z[G])\n   =a^2dx^2+\\mathcal P_z(G)\n        +\\sum_{r=L}^{2L}z^r\\mathcal R_r(G;x/z),\n \\qquad\n \\mathcal P_z(G)=\\gamma(G)+\\sum_{r=1}^{L-1}z^rE_r(G).\n \\label{eq:finite-metric-expansion}$$ Each $U_j$ has mean zero. The operators $U_j,E_r,\\mathcal R_r$ are smooth, supported on $\\overline D$, and polynomial in jets above order two, with smooth coefficients in the $2$-jet and position, and, where appropriate, $\\theta$. One may take the differential orders of $U_j$ and $E_r$ to be at most $2j$ and $2r+2$, respectively.",
        "source_id": "PM-333.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-periodic-expansion",

The operators $U_j,E_r,\\mathcal R_r$ are smooth, supported on $\\overline D$, and polynomial in jets above order two, with smooth coefficients in the $2$-jet and position, and, where appropriate, $\\theta$.         "exact_quote": "Let $(M,g)$ be a closed smooth Riemannian surface. There exist a smooth immersion $F^0:M\\to\\mathbb{R}^4$, a smooth unit normal $n^0$, and a finite list $$(D_j,x_j,y_j,a_j),\\qquad 1\\le j\\le K,$$ with the following properties. Each $D_j$ is a coordinate disk with smooth boundary $C_j$; $(x_j,y_j)$ are coordinates on a neighborhood of $\\overline D_j$; and $a_j$ is smooth, positive on $D_j$, and zero on its exterior. Set $Y_j=\\partial_{y_j}$ and $$\\gamma^j=\\gamma(F^0)+\\sum_{i=1}^j a_i^2(dx_i)^2,\n \\qquad \\gamma^0=\\gamma(F^0).$$ Then:*\n\n1.  *$\\gamma^K=g$, and $\\operatorname{Hess}_{\\gamma^{j-1}}x_j(Y_j,Y_j)>0$ on $\\overline D_j$.*\n\n2.  *The boundaries are pairwise transverse and have no triple intersection. For $i\\ne j$, $Y_j$ is tangent to $C_i$ at only finitely many points of $\\overline D_j$. At $C_i\\cap C_l\\cap D_j$, with $i,l,j$ distinct, both $dx_j(Y_i)$ and $dx_j(Y_l)$ are nonzero.*\n\n3.  *$B_{F^0}$ is nowhere the zero tensor, and $F^0,n^0$ satisfy all boundary conditions <a href=\"#eq:boundary-invariants\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:boundary-invariants\">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> for this list.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Closed smooth surface** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface: a compact smooth two-dimensional manifold without boundary.
- **Isometric immersion** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a smooth positive definite metric $g$ on $M$, an isometric immersion into Euclidean four-space is a smooth map $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ satisfying $$\langle dF_p(v),dF_p(w)\rangle=g_p(v,w) \qquad(p\in M,\ v,w\in T_pM).$$
- **Preferred normal** ([source](#sec:preliminaries)): A *preferred normal* is a smooth unit section of $N_F$.
- **Good covector** ([source](#stmt-auto-definition-1)): A nonzero covector $\xi\in T_p^*M$ is *good for $F$* if $B_F(Y,Y)\ne0$ for a nonzero vector $Y\in\ker\xi$.
- **Polynomial bound** ([source](#sec:preliminaries)): In the statement, a quantity is *polynomially bounded* if its absolute value is at most $Cz^{-A}$ for some finite $A\ge0$; the exponent may depend on a specified finite derivative order, but not on $z$.
- **Induced metric, normal bundle, and second fundamental form** ([source](#eq:gauss)): For a smooth immersion $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$, write $$\gamma(F)=F^*\delta_4, \qquad N_F=(dF(TM))^\perp, \qquad B_F(V,W)=\operatorname{proj}_{N_F}D_V(dF(W)).$$
- **Weighted norm** ([source](#eq:weighted-derivative)): For a smooth function, vector field, or tensor on the fixed coordinate atlas, set $$\|f\|_{m,s}=\sum_{j=0}^m s^j\\|f\\|_{C^j},\qquad 0<s\leq1.$$
- **Polynomial above order two** ([source](#eq:polynomial-jet-monomial)): A differential operator is called *polynomial above order two* if each of its coordinate components is a finite sum of terms $$\label{eq:polynomial-jet-monomial} c(x,j^2G)\prod_{\ell=1}^d\partial^{\alpha_\ell}G^{k_\ell}, \qquad \|\alpha_\ell\|>2,$$ where $c$ is smooth; the empty product is allowed.

| Q7 | Metric-error decay exponent | = | $(3k-18)/5$ | The cubic term after division by the smaller target amplitude; the current integer satisfies the stated lower bound. |
| Q8 | Polynomial perturbation exponent | = | $E+6$ | Differential perturbations polynomial above order two; variations through the stated order. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-spherical-preparation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spherical-preparation): Local spherical normalization with prescribed second jets. -> A spherical immersion with vanishing spherical second form at those points.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-global-preparation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-global-preparation): Positive primitive decomposition, convex phases, small cycles, and parameter transversality. -> An ordered primitive list with prescribed partial metrics and strict boundary conditions.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-small-increment) / [proof](#sec:small-modes): Finite mode solves, quadratic-mean adjustment, and oscillatory cancellation. -> A small displacement with finite-order metric error.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-prop-exact-correction) / [proof](#pm-h-17): Smoothing increments and increasing derivative-order blocks. -> An exact smooth immersion close in the second derivative norm.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-velocity-loop) / [proof](#sec:velocity-loop): A circular velocity, positive averaging density, and transverse turn control. -> A loop with the prescribed mean and leading metric.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-prop-primitive-realization) / [proof](#pm-h-25): Periodic correctors followed by slow mean correction and exact smoothing correction. -> An exact primitive metric realization with derivative asymptotics.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-prop-primitive-addition) / [proof](#sec:geometry-preservation): Exact Gauss identities, crossing estimates, and normalized normal interpolation. -> The later boundary conditions and global preferred normal are retained.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-40): Induction through the finite ordered primitive list. -> The target metric is induced by a smooth immersion.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: Every closed smooth Riemannian surface admits a smooth isometric immersion into Euclidean four-space.
- **I2**: Exact correction inside each primitive addition uses an infinite smoothing iteration.
- **I3**: A finite combination of distinct dilates preserves the integral and cancels the required moments.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Use the immersion as a collision-free physical surface. -> Self-intersections may occur.
- **A2**: Apply the global theorem to an open surface patch. -> Compactness and absence of boundary are not available.
- **A3**: Treat finite moment cancellation as a finite implementation of the exact immersion. -> The exact correction requires an infinite smoothing iteration.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The smoothing proof contains a finite Vandermonde coefficient construction for distinct positive dilation factors. Only these coefficients are implemented; the exact immersion still requires an infinite smoothing iteration.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON batch of lists of rational dilation factors.
# PRECONDITIONS: Each list is nonempty, positive and pairwise distinct.
# OUTPUTS: Exact rational moment-cancellation weights, or INVALID per list.
# TIME: Quadratic rational operations per list; bit cost depends on input size.
# SPACE: Linear rational storage per list plus the batch output.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact python-flint fmpq arithmetic; no floating-point operations.
# FAILURE: Invalid lists return INVALID; malformed JSON or rationals raise errors.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq
batch = json.load(sys.stdin)
result = []
for raw in batch:
    nodes = [fmpq(x) for x in raw]
    if not nodes or any(x <= 0 for x in nodes) or len(set(map(str,nodes))) != len(nodes):
        result.append("INVALID")
        continue
    weights = []
    for i, x in enumerate(nodes):
        weight = fmpq(1)
        for j, y in enumerate(nodes):
            if i != j:
                weight *= -y / (x - y)
        weights.append(str(weight))
    result.append(weights)
print(json.dumps(result))
```

    "oracle_code": "import sys, json\nfrom fractions import Fraction\ntry:\n    cases = json.load(open(sys.argv[1]))\n    answers = json.load(open(sys.argv[2]))\n    assert sys.argv[3] == \"MOMENTS\" and len(cases) == len(answers)\n    for raw, answer in zip(cases, answers):\n        b = tuple(Fraction(v) for v in raw)\n        valid = bool(b) and min(b) > 0 and len(set(b)) == len(b)\n        if not valid:\n            assert answer == \"INVALID\"\n            continue\n        assert isinstance(answer, list) and len(answer) == len(b)\n        c = tuple(Fraction(v) for v in answer)\n        for degree in range(len(b)):\n            residual = sum((ci * bi**degree for ci, bi in zip(c,b)), Fraction(0))\n            assert residual == (1 if degree == 0 else 0)\nexcept Exception:\n    sys.exit(1)\n",

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The main theorem concerns compact smooth surfaces without boundary. Blocked use: ['Extending the theorem to noncompact domains, surfaces with boundary, or nonsmooth discrete surfaces.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): The prescribed metric must be smooth and positive definite. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to degenerate, indefinite, or nonsmooth prescribed metrics.']
- **L3** (counterexample_scope): Immersion permits self-intersections; an embedding or collision-free realization is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Treating immersion existence as a guarantee of injectivity or absence of self-intersections.']
- **L4** (edge_case): Orientability and connectedness are not required. Blocked use: ['Rejecting a closed smooth surface solely because it is nonorientable or disconnected.']
- **L5** (nonconstructive): The finite list of primitive additions does not make the complete exact immersion a finite computational procedure: each addition uses an infinite smooth limiting iteration. Blocked use: ['Claiming that finite primitive assembly alone computes the exact immersion.', 'Promoting a finite moment-coefficient solve to a certificate for the complete immersion theorem.']
- **L6** (approximation): The exact correction provides proximity on the whole surface, without asserting that it vanishes outside a chosen support. Blocked use: ['Using the exact correction as a strictly support-preserving operation.']
- **L7** (approximation): Higher derivatives beyond the finite controlled input order may be arbitrarily large and can affect later waiting times. Blocked use: ['Inferring uniform control of all higher derivatives or uniform waiting times from the finite input assumptions.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): The correction theorem neither requires nor asserts regularity of the immersion family in its small parameter. Blocked use: ['Treating exact correction as a theorem about a smoothly parameterized family of corrected immersions.']
- **L9** (hypothesis): Moment cancellation does not imply positivity of the smoothing kernel. Blocked use: ['Using the moment-canceling kernel as a positivity-preserving averaging kernel without a separate argument.']
- **L10** (approximation): Finite-point spherical preparation uniformly controls only the first two derivatives; higher derivatives can grow as points approach each other. Blocked use: ['Inferring uniform bounds on all derivatives independent of the separation of preparation points.']
- **L11** (formal_scope): The small-mode construction does not require a global inverse of the linearized metric map. Blocked use: ['Attributing existence or a computational contract for a global linearized inverse to this construction.']
- **L12** (hypothesis): Euclidean four-space is the target of the main existence statement; the statement supplies no conclusion for a different ambient target. Blocked use: ['Replacing the prescribed Euclidean target by a lower-dimensional or indefinite ambient space.']
- **L13** (hypothesis): The primitive-addition proposition assumes a pointwise nonzero second fundamental form and a preferred normal, in addition to its phase and boundary conditions. Blocked use: ['Applying the primitive-addition step after checking only that the current map is immersive.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 333 (`lean/docs/333.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Smooth isometric immersions of closed surfaces into Euclidean four-space](../../preprints/Smooth-isometric-immersions-of-closed-surfaces-into-Euclidean-four-space-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves that every closed smooth Riemannian surface admits a smooth isometric immersion into $\mathbb R^4$. The differential preserves the Riemannian inner product on every tangent space. No orientability assumption is imposed, and the statement asks for an immersion rather than an embedding.

| declaration | kind |
|---|---|
| `Plane` | abbrev |
| `Space` | abbrev |
| `planeModel` | abbrev |
| `spaceModel` | abbrev |
| `SmoothMetric` | abbrev |
| `IsSmoothIsometricImmersion` | def |
| `smooth_isometric_immersion` | theorem |

Comparator source `SurfaceImmersion.lean` (1355 bytes, sha256 `668f6d90af60e4b3d0a61b37d0d6ecd0709e22ddb17bb185677c26fe19c92d37`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open Bundle Manifold
open scoped ContDiff

namespace ClosedSurfaceR4

abbrev Plane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)
abbrev Space := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 4)
abbrev planeModel := 𝓘(ℝ, Plane)
abbrev spaceModel := 𝓘(ℝ, Space)

section

variable (M : Type*) [TopologicalSpace M] [T2Space M]
  [SecondCountableTopology M] [CompactSpace M]
  [ChartedSpace Plane M] [IsManifold planeModel ∞ M]

abbrev SmoothMetric :=
  Bundle.ContMDiffRiemannianMetric planeModel ∞ Plane
    (fun p : M => TangentSpace planeModel p)

def IsSmoothIsometricImmersion (g : SmoothMetric M) (F : M → Space) : Prop :=
  ContMDiff planeModel spaceModel ∞ F ∧
  ∀ (p : M) (v w : TangentSpace planeModel p),
    inner ℝ (mfderiv planeModel spaceModel F p v)
      (mfderiv planeModel spaceModel F p w) = g.inner p v w

end

namespace FiniteOrderSmoothing

variable {M : Type*} [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace Plane M]
  [IsManifold planeModel ∞ M] [CompactSpace M] [T2Space M] [SecondCountableTopology M]

/-- Every compact smooth Riemannian surface admits a smooth isometric immersion into Euclidean four-space. -/
theorem smooth_isometric_immersion (g : SmoothMetric M) :
    ∃ F : M → Space, IsSmoothIsometricImmersion M g F := by
  sorry

end FiniteOrderSmoothing
end ClosedSurfaceR4

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Every closed smooth Riemannian surface admits a smooth isometric immersion into Euclidean four-space, without an orientability assumption. This resolves the closed-surface form of the classical four-dimensional isometric-immersion problem.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface: a compact smooth two-dimensional manifold without boundary. We do not assume that $M$ is orientable or connected. For a smooth positive definite metric $g$ on $M$, an isometric immersion into Euclidean four-space is a smooth map $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ satisfying $$\langle dF_p(v),dF_p(w)\rangle=g_p(v,w)
 \qquad(p\in M,\ v,w\in T_pM).$$ Positive definiteness then makes $dF_p$ injective at every point. The question is whether every such metric can be realized in this dimension.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Every closed smooth Riemannian surface $(M,g)$ admits a $C^\infty$ isometric immersion into $(\mathbb{R}^4,\delta_4)$, where $\delta_4=\sum_{i=1}^4 dx_i^2$.*

</div>

The result concerns immersion; self-intersections are allowed. There is no restriction on Gaussian curvature or on the topology of the closed surface. In particular, the construction applies directly to nonorientable surfaces.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context

Gromov explicitly recorded the question of smooth isometric immersion of arbitrary smooth Riemannian surfaces into $\mathbb{R}^4$ (Misha Gromov 2000, sec. I, Remark (d), p. 3 of the author version) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Gromov2000](#ref-Gromov2000)]. He later attributed the four-space surface question to Chern around 1950, while expressing uncertainty about the compactness qualification (Misha Gromov 2015, 4, footnote 6) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Gromov2015](#ref-Gromov2015)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> concerns the compact boundaryless case of this question.

Nash’s smooth embedding theorem gives a global realization of a compact $n$-dimensional Riemannian manifold in dimension $n(3n+11)/2$, which is $17$ for surfaces (Nash 1956, Theorem 2, p. 59) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Nash1956](#ref-Nash1956)]. A closer surface comparison is Gromov’s construction of smooth isometric immersions of closed surfaces into $\mathbb{R}^5$: his retrospective explicitly recalls this result and refers to §3.2.4 of *Partial Differential Relations* (Misha Gromov 2015, 59) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Gromov2015](#ref-Gromov2015)] <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GromovPDR1986](#ref-GromovPDR1986)](Mikhael Gromov 1986). Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> lowers this immersion dimension to four.

The local analytic theory reaches a smaller dimension. The theorem of Janet and Cartan places an analytic Riemannian surface locally and isometrically in $\mathbb{R}^3$ (Janet 1926; Cartan 1927) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Janet1926](#ref-Janet1926), [Cartan1927](#ref-Cartan1927)]; see also the formulation in (Misha Gromov 2015, sec. 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Gromov2015](#ref-Gromov2015)]. Both the analytic hypothesis and the local conclusion matter here. For comparison, the companion (OpenAI 2026, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[OpenAILocalObstruction2026](#ref-OpenAILocalObstruction2026)] constructs a smooth positive definite metric on $(-1,1)^2$ for which no neighborhood of the origin admits a smooth isometric immersion into $\mathbb{R}^3$.

There is also a smooth four-dimensional theory on planar domains. Poznyak proved that $C^{3,\alpha}$ data admit $C^{2,\alpha}$ isometric immersions into $\mathbb{R}^4$ on compact portions of a simply connected planar domain with regular boundary (Poznyak 1973, Theorem 4, p. 67) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Poznyak1973](#ref-Poznyak1973)]. The smooth version of this construction is stated and explained in Lewicka’s modern account (Lewicka 2026, Theorem 9.1, p. 57) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Lewicka2026v3](#ref-Lewicka2026v3)]. Passing from planar domains to a closed surface requires the corrections to remain compatible through a global construction. Here we achieve this by preserving the normal geometry needed for successive local metric corrections.

Regularity gives a second distinction. An immersion is *strictly short* if the prescribed metric minus its induced metric is positive definite. Nash’s oscillatory construction and Kuiper’s codimension-one refinement show that a strictly short immersion in positive codimension can be uniformly approximated by $C^1$ isometric immersions (Nash 1954; Kuiper 1955) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Nash1954](#ref-Nash1954), [Kuiper1955](#ref-Kuiper1955)]; the contemporaneous account (Choquet 1958, Theorems 1–2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Choquet1958](#ref-Choquet1958)] states the immersion form explicitly. Lewicka proves a finer flexibility theorem in $\mathbb{R}^4$ for metrics of low Hölder regularity. In the August 2026 version, the domain is bounded, planar, and diffeomorphic to a disk, the metric is $C^{r,\beta}$ on its closure with $0<r+\beta\le2$, and strictly short $C^1$ immersions admit uniform approximation by $C^{1,\alpha}$ isometric immersions for $0<\alpha<\min\{(r+\beta)/2,1\}$ (Lewicka 2026, Theorem 1.1, p. 2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Lewicka2026v3](#ref-Lewicka2026v3)]. These domain and regularity hypotheses differ from the smooth closed-surface statement of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

The proof uses the established ideas of positive rank-one metric decomposition, oscillatory metric correction, and smoothing to control derivative loss. We give the corresponding attribution where these ideas enter the construction. The mode equations, finite correctors, smooth convergence argument, and propagation of the normal geometry are proved below. Whitney’s ordinary immersion theorem supplies the starting map (Whitney 1944, Theorem 8, p. 274) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Whitney1944](#ref-Whitney1944)]; Sard’s equal-dimensional critical-value theorem is used to choose the support boundaries and phases (Sard 1942, Theorem 4.1, p. 885) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Sard1942](#ref-Sard1942)].

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The construction

<a id="eq:intro-sum"></a>
The proof has four stages. First prepare a smooth immersion into a small round three-sphere in $\mathbb{R}^4$ together with a finite decomposition of its metric deficit. Its induced metric $g_0$ is strictly below $g$, and the negative radial direction is a globally defined normal, including when $M$ is nonorientable. The decomposition has the form $$\label{eq:intro-sum}
 g-g_0=\sum_{j=1}^{J_0} a_j^2\,dx_j^2,$$ where each $x_j$ is a coordinate function on a disk and $a_j$ is smooth, positive in that disk, and zero outside it. The decomposition is arranged so that each phase has a positive transverse Hessian for the metric immediately preceding its addition.

Second, let $F$ be the current immersion and consider one addition $a^2dx^2$. Write $y$ for a complementary coordinate. The relevant plane is perpendicular to both $F_y$ and $F_{yy}$. A periodic path on a circle in this plane has the prescribed average and adds $a^2dx^2$ to the leading metric. Orthogonality to $F_{yy}$ removes the first transverse metric error. A finite expansion then cancels the oscillatory errors to an arbitrarily prescribed order. Its remaining mean terms form a finite differential perturbation of the induced-metric map. Small corrections on a slower scale reduce this perturbed map’s error to the required finite accuracy.

Third, turn the resulting approximate metric into an exact one. We prove a smooth correction theorem under the pointwise hypothesis that the vector-valued second fundamental form is nonzero. A finite construction solves the linearized equation on individual oscillatory modes to arbitrarily high finite accuracy, with suitably chosen phase directions. Smoothing between successive steps controls the lost derivatives; increasing the accuracy in successive blocks gives convergence in every smooth norm. No global inverse of the linearized metric map is required.

Finally, retain the geometric conditions needed for the remaining disks. The large oscillation may rotate the second fundamental form. Conditions on the later boundary curves, and a choice of large transverse derivatives at the turns of the velocity loop, prevent its relevant normal directions from vanishing or becoming opposite to the preferred normal. The small $C^2$ exact correction preserves the required derivative asymptotics. Together with the exact Gauss equation, these asymptotics establish the strict boundary and crossing conditions. After the finite list of additions, the induced metric is $g$ by <a href="#eq:intro-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-sum">[eq:intro-sum]</a>.

The two analytical devices used here have separate roles. The small-mode construction supplies high-order metric accuracy while its displacement is small in $C^2$. The large circle construction changes the metric by a prescribed positive primitive tensor while retaining selected second-derivative information. Their combination is what permits a finite global construction in codimension two. The number of global primitive additions is finite; within each addition, however, the exact correction uses an infinite smoothing iteration converging to one map in every $C^m$ norm.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> sets up normal geometry and admissible phases. Section <a href="#sec:primitive-statement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:primitive-statement">3</a> states the primitive-addition theorem, including the boundary conditions it preserves. Sections <a href="#sec:small-modes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:small-modes">4</a> and <a href="#sec:exact-correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:exact-correction">5</a> prove the small increment and exact correction results. Sections <a href="#sec:velocity-loop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:velocity-loop">6</a> and <a href="#sec:primitive-realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:primitive-realization">7</a> construct the velocity loop and realize the primitive metric. Section <a href="#sec:geometry-preservation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry-preservation">8</a> completes the primitive-addition proof. Section <a href="#sec:global-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:global-construction">9</a> prepares the finite list and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Normal geometry and admissible phases

We begin with the geometric information used by both corrections. A nonzero second fundamental form supplies enough directions for the small oscillations; it need not satisfy any rank condition beyond being nonzero as a tensor.

<a id="eq:gauss"></a>
Fix a smooth background metric and a finite collection of precompact coordinate patches on $M$. All $C^m$ norms and unmarked tangent norms use these fixed data. For a smooth immersion $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$, write $$\gamma(F)=F^*\delta_4,
 \qquad N_F=(dF(TM))^\perp,
 \qquad B_F(V,W)=\operatorname{proj}_{N_F}D_V(dF(W)).$$ The last expression is tensorial; in coordinates $B_{F,ij}$ is the normal projection of $F_{ij}$. Let $K_\gamma$ denote the Gaussian curvature of $\gamma=\gamma(F)$. Our sign convention for the Gauss equation is $$\label{eq:gauss}
 \langle B_F(V,V),B_F(W,W)\rangle-|B_F(V,W)|^2
 =K_\gamma\,|V\wedge W|_\gamma^2.$$ In coordinates $x,y$, the right side is $K_\gamma\det\gamma$ for $V=\partial_x,W=\partial_y$. The Hessian convention gives $\operatorname{Hess}_\gamma x(\partial_y,\partial_y)=-\Gamma^x_{yy}$. To check <a href="#eq:gauss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauss">[eq:gauss]</a>, choose Riemannian normal coordinates for $\gamma$, orthonormal at the point under consideration. There $\Gamma=0$, so $F_{xx},F_{xy},F_{yy}$ are normal vectors, and the coordinate curvature formula is $$K_\gamma=\partial_{xy}\gamma_{xy}
 -\tfrac12\bigl(\partial_{xx}\gamma_{yy}
                  +\partial_{yy}\gamma_{xx}\bigr).$$ Substituting $\gamma_{ij}=\langle F_i,F_j\rangle$ cancels the third derivatives and gives $K_\gamma=\langle F_{xx},F_{yy}\rangle-|F_{xy}|^2$ at that point. Tensoriality gives <a href="#eq:gauss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauss">[eq:gauss]</a> for arbitrary $V,W$.

A *preferred normal* is a smooth unit section of $N_F$. This is less than a normal frame: we will use complementary normal vectors only within coordinate disks. None of the following local constructions requires an orientation of $M$.

<a id="stmt-auto-definition-1"></a>
<div class="definition">

**Definition 2**. A nonzero covector $\xi\in T_p^*M$ is *good for $F$* if $B_F(Y,Y)\ne0$ for a nonzero vector $Y\in\ker\xi$. This condition is independent of the choice of $Y$ in that line.

</div>

If $B_F(p)\ne0$, there are only finitely many bad projective covectors at $p$. Indeed, take a normal component of $B_F(p)$ that is a nonzero scalar quadratic form. Its zeros in the projective tangent line are finite, and a zero of the vector-valued quadratic form must be one of these zeros.

We choose these good directions for the small oscillatory corrections. They need not be the phases $x_j$ of the prescribed primitive additions in <a href="#eq:intro-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-sum">[eq:intro-sum]</a>: those phases will satisfy a transverse Hessian condition, and their transverse second fundamental form may vanish in the interior of the support disk.

Writing a positive metric as a sum of positive rank-one forms is a basic part of Nash’s construction (Nash 1954) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Nash1954](#ref-Nash1954)]; see (Misha Gromov 2015, sec. 3.5.4, p. 32 and footnote 30) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Gromov2015](#ref-Gromov2015)] and (Källén 1978, Lemmas 2.2–2.3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Kallen1978](#ref-Kallen1978)] for related decompositions. Here the individual phases and their relevant pairwise combinations must also be good for the current second fundamental form. The following construction supplies those additional conditions.

<a id="stmt-lem-phase-data"></a>
<div id="lem:phase-data" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:phase-decomposition"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Positive phase decompositions). *Let $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ be a smooth immersion with $B_F(p)\ne0$ for every $p$, and let $h_0$ be a smooth positive definite tensor. There are finitely many functions $\phi_\nu$ defined on coordinate patches, smooth cutoffs $\psi_\nu$ supported compactly in those patches, and smooth fiberwise linear forms $q_\nu$ on symmetric tensors, such that $$\label{eq:phase-decomposition}
 H=\sum_\nu b_\nu(H)^2\,d\phi_\nu^2,
 \qquad b_\nu(H)=\psi_\nu\sqrt{q_\nu(H)}$$ whenever $H$ is sufficiently close to $h_0$ in $C^0$. Every square root has a strict positive lower bound before multiplication by the cutoff. On the appropriate supports the differentials of $\phi_\nu$, $2\phi_\nu$, and $\phi_\nu\pm\phi_\mu$ for $\nu\ne\mu$ are nonzero and good. These conclusions persist for immersions in a sufficiently small $C^2$ neighborhood of $F$. Each phase or phase combination has a smooth complementary coordinate coframe on the support where it is used.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-phase-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-phase-data</summary>

*Proof.* At a point choose an $h_0$-orthonormal coframe and three covectors with angles $0,\pi/3,2\pi/3$, rotated by a common angle. Their squares form a basis of the three-dimensional space of symmetric tensors, and $h_0$ is a positive linear combination of them. A rotation avoids the finite bad set simultaneously for all three directions. Extend the covectors as differentials of coordinate-linear functions on a small patch. The basis, positivity, and goodness conditions persist after shrinking the patch. The three coefficient forms in this basis are smooth in position. A finite such cover and a partition $\sum_\alpha\psi_\alpha^2=1$, with each cutoff supported in its patch, give <a href="#eq:phase-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-decomposition">[eq:phase-decomposition]</a>, using three indices over each patch.

It remains to control combinations from different indices. Multiply successive phases by sufficiently rapidly increasing positive constants, and divide their coefficient forms by the squares of those constants. For two intersecting supports, the differential of either sum or difference, after normalization, is arbitrarily close to that of the member with the larger weight. There are only finitely many supports, and goodness has a positive angular margin on their compact closures. Inductively chosen weights therefore make every required combination good and nonzero. Doubles and signs change no kernel line. A coordinate complementary to the dominant phase remains complementary to the combination. All conditions are strict conditions on the first two jets of the immersion; compactness gives their persistence. ◻

</details>

The small-oscillation argument is local on these coframes. If a combination is not globally injective as a coordinate function, the dual coordinate vector fields still make sense where its differential and the chosen complement are independent. Subdivision gives actual coordinate neighborhoods when necessary. Amplitudes are compactly supported there, so extension by zero is smooth.

For the exact correction, the phase data must also be controlled when the input immersion varies with a small parameter. We record this quantitative version separately. In the statement, a quantity is *polynomially bounded* if its absolute value is at most $Cz^{-A}$ for some finite $A\ge0$; the exponent may depend on a specified finite derivative order, but not on $z$.

<a id="stmt-lem-polynomial-phase-data"></a>
<div id="lem:polynomial-phase-data" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Phase data for a varying family). *Fix a metric $h_*$ and suppose that $f_z$, $0<z<z_0$, are smooth immersions satisfying uniform upper and positive lower metric bounds, $$\|f_z\|_{C^j}=O(z^{-j})\quad(0\le j\le3),
 \qquad
 \inf_{p\in M}\max_{|Y|=1}|B_{f_z}(Y,Y)|\ge c>0.$$ Then the phase data of Lemma <a href="#lem:phase-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phase-data">3</a>, with $h_0=h_*$, can be chosen on cells of diameter comparable to $z^6$. The number of cells and all fixed-order derivatives of their cutoff and phase data are polynomially bounded. The good-direction estimates and metric nondegeneracy persist throughout the $C^2$ ball of radius $z^4$ about $f_z$, for all sufficiently small $z$.*

*No regularity with respect to $z$ is required. The construction uses a bounded number of phase weights, independent of the number of cells. The inverse coframes and the reciprocals of the relevant second-form lengths have polynomial bounds. Consequently every fixed finite differential construction formed from these data by sums, products, derivatives, metric inversion, normal projection, and division by the stated nonzero quantities has polynomial bounds, provided its input has the required finite polynomial derivative bounds. Its exponents depend only on the derivative orders and on the input exponents, not on their implicit constants.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-phase-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-polynomial-phase-data</summary>

*Proof.* Metric bounds imply $df_z=O(1)$. Differentiating the formula for normal projection gives $$B_{f_z}=O(z^{-2}),\qquad DB_{f_z}=O(z^{-4}).$$ For example, $D\operatorname{proj}_{N_{f_z}}=O(z^{-2})$ and multiplication by $D^2f_z=O(z^{-2})$ gives the latter exponent; the $D^3f_z$ term is smaller. The constants depend only on the fixed background data, metric bounds, and specified family bounds.

At each cell center normalize the second fundamental form to have norm one. The space of normalized normal-valued symmetric forms, in background orthonormal frames, is compact. For each such form a rotated positive triple avoids all its bad directions. Thus the maximum, over rotations, of the least of the three second-form lengths has a strictly positive lower bound over this compact set. Shrinking a uniform angular neighborhood preserves a fixed fraction of this bound. This supplies a uniform angular margin and an estimate $$|B_{f_z}(Y,Y)|\ge\kappa |B_{f_z}|$$ for normalized kernel vectors in the selected directions, with $\kappa>0$ independent of the cell. Fixed coordinate-frame comparisons only alter $\kappa$ by a uniform factor.

Use grids of mesh $z^6$ in the fixed charts, restricted to fixed compact subpatches covering $M$. Smooth cutoffs equal to one on the covering cells and supported in their fixed enlargements have derivatives $O(z^{-6j})$ of order $j$. Divide them by the square root of their sum of squares. This gives a partition by squares with the same type of bounds. A fixed lattice coloring, combined with the finite chart index, gives a bounded number of colors such that enlarged supports of the same color are disjoint. The number of cells is $O(z^{-12})$.

On an enlarged cell the second form varies by $O(z^2)$; its nonzero lower bound makes this variation small relative to its length. The center’s phase triple therefore stays good. The fixed smooth tensor $h_*$ varies by $O(z^6)$, so the positive basis decomposition has a uniform positivity margin. Assign one successive weight to each color and each place in the triple. Intersecting supports have different such labels. Since the angular margins are uniform, a fixed finite sequence of dominant weights makes every pairwise combination good. Derivatives of the resulting coframes and their inverses are bounded at every fixed order by the fixed charts and these weights. No weight depends on the total number of cells.

If $\|G-f_z\|_{C^2}\le z^4$, projection onto the tangent and normal spaces changes by $O(z^4)$. Hence $$|B_G-B_{f_z}|\le C z^4(1+z^{-2})=O(z^2),$$ so the direction and metric margins persist. Local unit normals used in mode solves are obtained by normalizing a perpendicular product of two tangent vectors and a nonzero vector $B_G(Y,Y)$. Their denominators are bounded below by the metric and direction margins. The coefficient forms in <a href="#eq:phase-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:phase-decomposition">[eq:phase-decomposition]</a> retain positive arguments for their square roots on a fixed $C^0$ neighborhood of $h_*$. Product and chain rules now prove the last assertion: finitely many inversions and differentiations multiply only finitely many polynomial bounds. The polynomial number of summands can be absorbed in another finite exponent. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:primitive-statement"></a>
### The primitive metric addition

The global construction will add finitely many positive tensors of rank one. We first state the local step, including the boundary conditions that allow one step to be followed by the next. Only a preferred unit normal is propagated; a full normal frame is chosen locally when needed.

<a id="eq:crossing-invariant"></a>
<a id="eq:crossing-gauss-interpretation"></a>
<a id="eq:boundary-invariants"></a>
Let $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ be a smooth immersion, and write $\gamma=\gamma(F)$. Suppose $C_0,\ldots,C_m$ are smooth embedded closed curves with nonzero smooth vector fields $Y_i$ defined on neighborhoods of $C_i$. The fields $Y_i$ are not required to be tangent to the curves; in the application they are the transverse coordinate directions of later phases. Set $$A_i(F)=B_F(Y_i,Y_i)\quad\hbox{on }C_i.$$ For $i\ne l$, wherever the curves intersect and $A_i(F)\ne0$, define $$Q_{il}(F)=
 \left\langle B_F(Y_i,Y_l),\frac{A_i(F)}{|A_i(F)|}\right\rangle^2
 +K_{\gamma}|Y_i\wedge Y_l|_{\gamma}^2.
 \label{eq:crossing-invariant}$$ Here $K_\gamma$ is Gauss curvature, and $|u\wedge v|_\gamma^2=\gamma(u,u)\gamma(v,v)-\gamma(u,v)^2$. The Gauss equation gives a useful interpretation. With $\widehat A_i=A_i(F)/|A_i(F)|$ and $Z_{il}=B_F(Y_i,Y_l)$, $$Q_{il}(F)=\langle A_i(F),A_l(F)\rangle
       -\bigl|Z_{il}-\langle Z_{il},\widehat A_i\rangle\widehat A_i\bigr|^2.
 \label{eq:crossing-gauss-interpretation}$$ Thus $Q_{il}>0$ forces the two pure second-form vectors to have positive inner product. It also shows that $Q_{il}$ depends continuously on the $2$-jet of $F$ wherever $A_i\ne0$, despite its curvature expression in <a href="#eq:crossing-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crossing-invariant">[eq:crossing-invariant]</a>. Both observations will be used when the preferred normal is joined across the support boundary. For a preferred normal $n$, the conditions to be preserved are $$\begin{aligned}
 &A_i(F)\ne0,\qquad A_i(F)/|A_i(F)|\ne-n
 &&\text{on }C_i,\\
 &\langle A_i(F),n\rangle>0,\qquad Q_{il}(F)>0
 &&\text{on }C_i\cap C_l\quad(i\ne l).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:boundary-invariants}$$ The crossing conditions are imposed in both orders. In particular they require positivity of the dot product with $n$ for each of the two pure second-form vectors. The numbers $Q_{il}$ do not depend on the chosen preferred normal.

<a id="stmt-prop-primitive-addition"></a>
<div id="prop:primitive-addition" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:primitive-hessian"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Primitive addition with preserved boundary conditions). *Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface and $F:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ a smooth immersion whose second fundamental form is nonzero as a tensor at every point. Assume $F$ has a preferred normal $n$. Let $D$ be a disk with smooth boundary $C_0$ and coordinates $(x,y)$ on a neighborhood of $\overline D$. Let $a\in C^\infty(M)$ be nonnegative, positive exactly on $D$, and put $Y_0=\partial_y$. Suppose $$\operatorname{Hess}_{\gamma(F)}x(\partial_y,\partial_y)>0
 \quad\text{on }\overline D.
