 \label{eq:primitive-hessian}$$ Let $C_1,\ldots,C_m$ be finitely many later curves with nonzero smooth vector fields $Y_i$ on their neighborhoods. Assume the following:*

1.  *All curves $C_0,\ldots,C_m$ are pairwise transverse and have no triple intersection.*

2.  *On the portion of each later curve $C_i$, $1\le i\le m$, in $\overline D$, the vector $\partial_y$ is tangent to $C_i$ at only finitely many points.*

3.  *At every $C_i\cap C_l\cap D$ with distinct $i,l>0$, $dx(Y_i)\ne0$ and $dx(Y_l)\ne0$.*

4.  *Conditions <a href="#eq:boundary-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-invariants">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> hold for all indices $0,\ldots,m$.*

*Then, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there are a smooth immersion $F_+:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ and a preferred normal $n_+$ such that $$\gamma(F_+)=\gamma_+:=\gamma(F)+a^2\,dx^2,
 \qquad B_{F_+}\ne0\ \text{at every point},$$ conditions <a href="#eq:boundary-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-invariants">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a> hold for $(F_+,n_+)$ and all later indices $1,\ldots,m$, and $$\|F_+-F\|_{C^2(M\setminus D)}<\varepsilon.$$ The tensor $a^2dx^2$ is extended by zero outside the coordinate neighborhood.*

</div>

Because $a$ is smooth and vanishes outside $D$, it and all its derivatives vanish on $C_0$. This makes the extended tensor smooth. The proposition allows the map itself to change on the exterior; the stated $C^2$ control is what will preserve the boundary data of the later steps.

The hypotheses have different roles. Table <a href="#tab:invariants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:invariants">1</a> records how they are established initially and passed to the next primitive step. In particular, the phase-Hessian condition depends on the current metric, whereas the boundary conditions also depend on the immersion and normal.

<div id="tab:invariants">

| Condition                                                            | Initial construction                                      | Use                                                    | Preservation                                                       |
|:---------------------------------------------------------------------|:----------------------------------------------------------|:-------------------------------------------------------|:-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| $B_F\ne0$ everywhere                                                 | Radial component of a spherical immersion                 | Good phases and exact correction                       | Nonzero transverse second form on the disk; exterior $C^2$ control |
| Global unit normal $n$                                               | Negative radial section                                   | Choice of velocity-plane frame and boundary directions | Local adjustments, projection and normalized interpolation         |
| Positive transverse phase Hessian                                    | Convex phases and sufficiently many finite cycles         | Independence of $F_y,F_{yy}$                           | Each actual partial metric is prescribed in advance                |
| $A_i\ne0$ and $A_i/|A_i|\ne-n$                                       | Positive radial projection                                | Velocity frame near each later support boundary        | Gauss identity and the turn construction                           |
| Positive normal projections and both ordered $Q_{il}>0$ at crossings | Second form in $S^3$ made zero at the finite crossing set | Collar estimates and successive boundary control       | Mixed–pure comparison and local normal adjustments                 |

The geometric data carried through the finite construction. Section <a href="#sec:global-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:global-construction">9</a> supplies the initial data and verifies the phase-Hessian condition for every prescribed partial metric. Sections <a href="#sec:velocity-loop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:velocity-loop">6</a>–<a href="#sec:geometry-preservation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry-preservation">8</a> preserve the immersion and normal conditions. A single unit normal suffices.

</div>

We prove the proposition in three parts. After the analytic correction results, Section <a href="#sec:velocity-loop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:velocity-loop">6</a> constructs a periodic velocity whose averaged displacement is zero and whose leading metric adds $a^2dx^2$. Section <a href="#sec:primitive-realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:primitive-realization">7</a> realizes this velocity by exact smooth immersions with controlled derivatives. Section <a href="#sec:geometry-preservation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry-preservation">8</a> proves the boundary conditions and constructs the new global preferred normal.

<a id="sec:small-modes"></a>
### Small oscillatory metric increments

We first construct a correction whose amplitude and wavelength are both small. A good phase, in the sense of Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>, permits an oscillation in the normal direction perpendicular to its transverse second form. Finite differential corrections make this oscillation solve the homogeneous linearized equation to high order. Its leading quadratic mean is a positive rank-one tensor. Combining finitely many such oscillations will add a prescribed positive tensor to finite accuracy while changing the map by a quantity small in $C^2$.

We carry out the construction for the metric map and for a finite polynomial differential perturbation of it. In Section <a href="#sec:primitive-realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:primitive-realization">7</a>, removing the large velocity loop’s oscillating errors leaves mean terms that are differential operators of the slow map, multiplied by powers of the fast scale. Here we allow those terms as part of the operator being corrected. The perturbation changes the required relation between scales. We will bound it using a power of the short scale that is independent of the requested finite accuracy.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Weighted bounds and the increment statement

<a id="eq:weighted-derivative"></a>
For a smooth function, vector field, or tensor on the fixed coordinate atlas, set $$|f|_{m,s}=\sum_{j=0}^m s^j\|f\|_{C^j},\qquad 0<s\leq1.$$ For a map $G$, the notation $|j^2G|_{m,s}$ means $\sum_{j=0}^m s^j\|G\|_{C^{j+2}}$. We write $f=O_s(b)$ if, for every fixed $m$, $|f|_{m,s}\leq C_m b$. In statements concerning a family of inputs, the constants may depend on its indicated weighted bounds and on the fixed coordinate data. Product estimates and smooth composition estimates hold in these norms by the product and chain rules. In particular, $$\label{eq:weighted-derivative}
 |\partial^rG|_{m,s}\leq C_m s^{2-r}
 \quad(r\geq2),\qquad\text{if }j^2G=O_s(1).$$

<a id="eq:small-positive-decomposition"></a>
Fix positive phase data from Lemma <a href="#lem:phase-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:phase-data">3</a>: phases $\phi_\nu$, cutoffs $\psi_\nu$, and linear forms $q_\nu$. Choose concentric $C^0$ balls $\mathcal U_0,\mathcal U_1$ about a metric $h_0$, with radii $0<r_0<r_1$, so that for $A\in\mathcal U_1$, $$\label{eq:small-positive-decomposition}
 b_\nu(A)=\psi_\nu\sqrt{q_\nu(A)},\qquad
 A=\sum_\nu b_\nu(A)^2\,d\phi_\nu^2.$$ All square-root arguments have a uniform positive lower bound for $A\in\mathcal U_1$ on their coordinate neighborhoods. The prescribed tensor belongs to $\mathcal U_0$; the larger ball allows the finite intermediate tensor adjustments below. The differentials of $\phi_\nu$, $2\phi_\nu$, and $\phi_\nu\pm\phi_\mu$ are good wherever the corresponding supports meet. For each such phase choose local coordinates with that phase as the first coordinate. All these finitely many coframes and their inverses have fixed bounds. We require the relevant immersion 2-jets to remain in a compact subset of the open set where these conditions hold.

<a id="eq:polynomial-jet-monomial"></a>
A differential operator is called *polynomial above order two* if each of its coordinate components is a finite sum of terms $$\label{eq:polynomial-jet-monomial}
 c(x,j^2G)\prod_{\ell=1}^d\partial^{\alpha_\ell}G^{k_\ell},
 \qquad |\alpha_\ell|>2,$$ where $c$ is smooth; the empty product is allowed. This definition permits arbitrary smooth dependence on the low jets within the prescribed open set.

<a id="stmt-prop-small-increment"></a>
<div id="prop:small-increment" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:small-increment-estimate"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Small increment). *Let the phase data above be fixed. Let $P_1,\ldots,P_d$ be fixed smooth tensor-valued differential operators polynomial above order two, and put $$\mathcal P(G)=\gamma(G)+\sum_{i=1}^d\epsilon^i P_i(G),
 \qquad 0\leq\epsilon\leq1.$$ There is a finite exponent $p$, depending on these operators and independent of the integer $q$ below, with the following property. Suppose $G$ is an immersion whose 2-jets satisfy the stated compact margins, $H\in\mathcal U_0$, and $$j^2G=O_s(1),\qquad H=O_s(1).$$ For every positive integer $q$, every $\delta>0$, and scales $0<\tau\leq s\leq1$, define $$\eta=\frac\tau s+\epsilon\tau^{-p}.$$ If $\eta$ and $\delta/\tau$ are sufficiently small, there is a smooth displacement $U$ such that $$\label{eq:small-increment-estimate}
 U=O_\tau(\delta\tau),\qquad
 \mathcal P(G+U)-\mathcal P(G)-\delta^2H
   =O_\tau\left(\delta\eta^q+\frac{\delta^3}{\tau}\right).$$ The smallness thresholds and every fixed output norm use only finitely many weighted input bounds. Their required orders may increase with $q$. Each local summand of $U$ is supported in an original phase support or an intersection of two such supports. If the perturbation sum is absent, take $\eta=\tau/s$.*

</div>

The proof has three finite steps: solve the nonzero linear modes to high order, adjust their quadratic mean, and cancel the remaining quadratic modes. There is no infinite series of differential operators in this construction.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The conjugated linearized equation

Fix one permitted phase and call its coordinates $x,y$. Complexify all vector spaces linearly. Let $g=\gamma(G)$ and $D_G=\gamma'(G)$. Decompose a complex amplitude $Z$ as $$Z=G_k g^{k\ell}u_\ell+v,
 \qquad u_i=\langle G_i,Z\rangle,
 \qquad v\perp\operatorname{span}(G_x,G_y).$$ Since $G_{ij}=\Gamma^k_{ij}G_k+B_{ij}$, direct differentiation gives $$(D_GZ)_{ij}
 =\partial_i u_j+\partial_j u_i-2\Gamma^k_{ij}u_k
       -2\langle B_{ij},v\rangle.$$ Here and in this subsection $B=B_G$. This identity already accounts for the normal component; no derivative of the normal frame is omitted. Conjugating by $e^{ix/\tau}$ gives <a id="eq:conjugated-metric-linearization"></a>$$\label{eq:conjugated-metric-linearization}
 (D_G^xZ)_{ij}
 =\partial_i u_j+\partial_j u_i-2\Gamma^k_{ij}u_k
 +\frac{i}{\tau}(\delta_{ix}u_j+\delta_{jx}u_i)
 -2\langle B_{ij},v\rangle.$$

<a id="eq:normal-elimination"></a>
The good-phase condition says that $b=B_{yy}$ does not vanish. Given a target amplitude $h$ and a normal amplitude $v_0$ perpendicular to $b$, define $$\label{eq:normal-elimination}
 c_{ij}=\frac{\langle B_{ij},b\rangle}{|b|^2},\qquad
 v=v_0+\frac b{|b|^2}
 \left(\partial_yu_y-\Gamma^k_{yy}u_k-\frac{h_{yy}}2\right).$$ The $yy$ equation in $D_G^xZ=h$ is then exact. The $xx$ equation, divided by 2, and the $xy$ equation become <a id="eq:conjugated-xx"></a><a id="eq:conjugated-xy"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac i\tau u_x+\partial_xu_x-\Gamma^k_{xx}u_k
  -c_{xx}(\partial_yu_y-\Gamma^k_{yy}u_k)
 &=\frac{h_{xx}}2+\langle B_{xx},v_0\rangle
                      -\frac{c_{xx}h_{yy}}2,
 \label{eq:conjugated-xx}\\
 \frac i\tau u_y+\partial_xu_y+\partial_yu_x-2\Gamma^k_{xy}u_k
  -2c_{xy}(\partial_yu_y-\Gamma^k_{yy}u_k)
 &=h_{xy}+2\langle B_{xy},v_0\rangle-c_{xy}h_{yy}.
 \label{eq:conjugated-xy}
\end{aligned}$$ Thus the remaining system is $$\frac i\tau u+Lu=k(h,v_0),$$ where $L$ is first order and $k$ is algebraic and linear. All coefficients are smooth functions of the 2-jet of $G$ on the permitted compact set.

<a id="eq:first-parametrix-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:first-parametrix-error"></a>
Define $S(h,v_0)$ by taking $u=(\tau/i)k(h,v_0)$ and reconstructing $v,Z$ from <a href="#eq:normal-elimination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normal-elimination">[eq:normal-elimination]</a>. Weighted differentiation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 |S(h,v_0)|_{m,s}
 &\leq C_m\bigl(|h|_{m+1,s}+|v_0|_{m+1,s}\bigr),
 \label{eq:first-parametrix-bound}\\
 |D_G^xS(h,v_0)-h|_{m,s}
 &\leq C_m\frac\tau s
      \bigl(|h|_{m+1,s}+|v_0|_{m+1,s}\bigr).
 \label{eq:first-parametrix-error}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed the residual is $Lu$, and the reconstructed normal component contains at most one derivative of $u$. In the homogeneous case the leading term is exactly the chosen normal amplitude: <a id="eq:free-mode-leading"></a>$$\label{eq:free-mode-leading}
 |S(0,v_0)-v_0|_{m,s}
 \leq C_m\frac\tau s|v_0|_{m+1,s}.$$ These are differential formulas, so they preserve supports.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A fixed exponent for polynomial perturbations

We next quantify the effect of the operators $P_i$. This is the point at which independence from the approximation order $q$ is needed.

<a id="eq:fixed-perturbation-power"></a>
For a monomial <a href="#eq:polynomial-jet-monomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial-jet-monomial">[eq:polynomial-jet-monomial]</a>, let $$E_\alpha=\sum_{\ell=1}^d(|\alpha_\ell|-2),
 \qquad E=\max E_\alpha,$$ where the maximum runs over all monomials of all $P_i$. For an empty collection take $E=0$. A permissible choice will be $$\label{eq:fixed-perturbation-power}
 p=E+6.$$

To verify this, consider a variation of order $k\leq3$. Suppose $h$ arguments replace higher-jet factors of orders $r_1,\ldots,r_h$ and the other $k-h$ arguments differentiate the smooth low-jet coefficient. The unvaried factors have excess weight $E_\alpha-\sum_{j=1}^h(r_j-2)$. The variation arguments carry at most $\sum_jr_j+2(k-h)$ derivatives. The total short-scale power is therefore at most $$E_\alpha-\sum_{j=1}^h(r_j-2)+\sum_{j=1}^hr_j+2(k-h)
 =E_\alpha+2k\leq E+6.$$ Weighted differentiation does not increase this exponent: <a href="#eq:weighted-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-derivative">[eq:weighted-derivative]</a> controls each differentiated higher-jet factor, and the chain rule controls the smooth low-jet coefficient. For conjugated amplitudes, each derivative of an exponential costs a power of $\tau^{-1}$, while a slow derivative costs $s^{-1}\leq\tau^{-1}$.

<a id="eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound"></a>
Put $\mathcal R=\mathcal P-\gamma$. For $1\leq k\leq3$, this proves, with a fixed finite derivative loss $\ell$, the bounds $$\begin{aligned}
 &\left|e^{-i(\phi_1+\cdots+\phi_k)/\tau}
 \mathcal R^{(k)}(G)
 \bigl(e^{i\phi_1/\tau}Z_1,\ldots,e^{i\phi_k/\tau}Z_k\bigr)
 \right|_{m,s}
 \notag\\[-1mm]
 &\hspace{35mm}\leq
 C_m\epsilon\tau^{-p}\prod_{j=1}^k|Z_j|_{m+\ell,s}.
 \label{eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ The phases in this estimate belong to the fixed finite collection. At scale $\tau$, the same estimate without exponentials holds for arbitrary variations at every map $K$ with $j^2K=O_\tau(1)$ in the permitted low-jet domain. The constants use finitely many corresponding weighted bounds. The inequality $\epsilon^i\leq\epsilon$ handles all the perturbation coefficients.

<a id="eq:finite-mode-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:finite-mode-error"></a>
<a id="eq:finite-free-mode"></a>
Let $\mathcal L=(\mathcal P'(G))^x$ and $S_0(h)=S(h,0)$. Combining <a href="#eq:first-parametrix-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-parametrix-bound">[eq:first-parametrix-bound]</a>–<a href="#eq:first-parametrix-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-parametrix-error">[eq:first-parametrix-error]</a> with <a href="#eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound">[eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound]</a> gives $$|\mathcal L S(h,v_0)-h|_{m,s}
 \leq C_m\eta\bigl(|h|_{m+\ell,s}+|v_0|_{m+\ell,s}\bigr),$$ after increasing $\ell$. Starting with $Z_0=S(h,v_0)$, define $$r_j=\mathcal LZ_j-h,\qquad Z_{j+1}=Z_j-S_0(r_j).$$ The exact identity $$r_{j+1}=-(\mathcal L S_0-\operatorname{Id})r_j$$ shows by finite induction that, for any integer $q$, the resulting amplitude $Z$ satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 |Z|_{m,s}&\leq C_{m,q}
       \bigl(|h|_{m+\ell_q,s}+|v_0|_{m+\ell_q,s}\bigr),
 \label{eq:finite-mode-bound}\\
 |\mathcal LZ-h|_{m,s}&\leq C_{m,q}\eta^q
       \bigl(|h|_{m+\ell_q,s}+|v_0|_{m+\ell_q,s}\bigr).
 \label{eq:finite-mode-error}
\end{aligned}$$ For $h=0$ it also satisfies $$\label{eq:finite-free-mode}
 |Z-v_0|_{m,s}\leq C_{m,q}\eta|v_0|_{m+\ell_q,s}.$$ The integer $\ell_q$ and the constants may increase with $q$, but the exponent $p$ in <a href="#eq:fixed-perturbation-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-perturbation-power">[eq:fixed-perturbation-power]</a> has already been fixed. Every step is differential and support preserving.

We have now solved every allowed nonzero mode to any fixed power of $\eta$. It remains to select the free amplitudes so that their quadratic mean is the desired tensor and then remove the oscillatory quadratic error.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The quadratic mean and its finite adjustment

Finite inner corrections to oscillatory amplitudes also occur in Källén’s low-regularity embedding construction (Källén 1978, Lemma 3.3 and its proof) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Kallen1978](#ref-Kallen1978)]. We derive the mean equation and its finite substitution estimates for the differential operators considered here.

In a phase chart choose a smooth unit normal $n_\nu$ perpendicular to $(B_G)_{yy}$. One explicit choice is the normalized ambient perpendicular product of $G_x,G_y,(B_G)_{yy}$. The vectors in this product are independent. The choice depends smoothly on the 2-jet and uses only the orientation of this chart and of $\mathbb{R}^4$.

<a id="eq:free-sum-linear-error"></a>
For a trial tensor $A\in\mathcal U_1$, use $$v_{0,\nu}=\sqrt2\,\delta\tau b_\nu(A)n_\nu,\qquad h=0,$$ in the finite mode construction, and denote its amplitude by $Z_\nu(A)$. Define the real displacement $$U_1(A)=\sum_\nu\operatorname{Re}
             \bigl(e^{i\phi_\nu/\tau}Z_\nu(A)\bigr).$$ Then $U_1=O_\tau(\delta\tau)$. Before multiplication by the exponential, the leading complex amplitude of $dU_1/\delta$ in the $\nu$th mode is $$i\sqrt2\,b_\nu(A)n_\nu\,d\phi_\nu,$$ with error $O_s(\eta)$. This follows from <a href="#eq:finite-free-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-free-mode">[eq:finite-free-mode]</a>; a slow derivative of the leading amplitude contributes $O_s(\tau/s)$. Furthermore, $$\label{eq:free-sum-linear-error}
 \mathcal P'(G)U_1=O_\tau(\delta\tau\eta^q).$$

Expand the quadratic tensor $$Q(A)=\tfrac12\mathcal P''(G)(U_1(A),U_1(A))$$ using the positive and negative mode labels. Its *zero-phase part*, denoted $Q_0(A)$, consists of a mode paired with its own complex conjugate. All other terms have phases $\pm2\phi_\nu$ or $\pm\phi_\nu\pm\phi_\mu$ with $\nu\ne\mu$. The latter phases have nonzero differentials on their supports, by hypothesis; in particular they do not contribute another zero-phase term. Repeated nonzero phases may simply be grouped.

<a id="eq:quadratic-mean-map"></a>
For one leading real mode, the metric quadratic term is $$2\delta^2b_\nu(A)^2\sin^2(\phi_\nu/\tau)\,d\phi_\nu^2
 =\delta^2b_\nu(A)^2
       \bigl(1-\cos(2\phi_\nu/\tau)\bigr)\,d\phi_\nu^2.$$ Its zero-phase term is $\delta^2b_\nu(A)^2d\phi_\nu^2$. Thus <a href="#eq:small-positive-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-positive-decomposition">[eq:small-positive-decomposition]</a> and the amplitude error give $$\label{eq:quadratic-mean-map}
 \delta^{-2}Q_0(A)=A+T(A),\qquad T(A)=O_s(\eta).$$ The contribution of $\mathcal R''$ has the same bound, since <a href="#eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound">[eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound]</a> bounds it by $C\epsilon\tau^{-p}(\delta\tau)^2\leq C\delta^2\eta$. Every nonzero-mode coefficient of $Q$ is $O_s(\delta^2)$.

<a id="eq:mean-map-difference"></a>
The map $T$ has a finite derivative-loss difference estimate: $$\label{eq:mean-map-difference}
 |T(A)-T(\bar A)|_{m,s}
 \leq C_m\eta|A-\bar A|_{m+L_q,s}$$ on bounded sets of weighted norms in $\mathcal U_1$. To see this, the square roots in $b_\nu$ have positive lower bounds, so their chain-rule difference estimates apply. The finite mode construction is linear in $b_\nu(A)n_\nu$. In the difference of the two sides of <a href="#eq:quadratic-mean-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-mean-map">[eq:quadratic-mean-map]</a>, the leading contribution $A-\bar A$ cancels exactly. Every remaining bilinear term contains either one $O_s(\eta)$ mode error or the factor $\epsilon\tau^{-p}$ from the perturbation. The product rule then gives <a href="#eq:mean-map-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mean-map-difference">[eq:mean-map-difference]</a> with a finite $L_q$.

<a id="eq:adjusted-quadratic-mean"></a>
Set $A_0=H$ and successively define $$A_{j+1}=H-T(A_j).$$ For any fixed number of substitutions, all these tensors remain in $\mathcal U_1$ and have bounded weighted norms when $\eta$ is sufficiently small. The positive gap $r_1-r_0$ and the estimate $T(A_j)=O_s(\eta)$ give this domain condition using only finitely many input derivatives: for a fixed number of substitutions, work backward through their finite derivative losses to obtain the required $C^0$ bounds. Once all iterates lie in $\mathcal U_1$, the finite formulas give bounds in each higher weighted norm by the chain rule, without a further domain restriction. Their successive differences gain one power of $\eta$ by <a href="#eq:mean-map-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mean-map-difference">[eq:mean-map-difference]</a>. More explicitly, for $j\geq1$, $$A_j+T(A_j)-H=T(A_j)-T(A_{j-1})=O_s(\eta^{j+1}).$$ After finitely many substitutions, choose the resulting $A$ so that $$\label{eq:adjusted-quadratic-mean}
 Q_0(A)=\delta^2H+O_s(\delta^2\eta^q).$$ This is a finite algebraic procedure on smooth tensors, not a contraction argument in a function space with derivative loss.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Cancellation and the cubic remainder

<a id="eq:quadratic-cancellation"></a>
Use the amplitude $A$ just obtained. On each nonzero quadratic phase, apply <a href="#eq:finite-mode-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-mode-bound">[eq:finite-mode-bound]</a>–<a href="#eq:finite-mode-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-mode-error">[eq:finite-mode-error]</a> with target the negative corresponding coefficient of $Q-Q_0$ and with $v_0=0$. Taking real parts gives $U_2$ such that $$\label{eq:quadratic-cancellation}
 U_2=O_\tau(\delta^2),\qquad
 \mathcal P'(G)U_2+(Q-Q_0)=O_\tau(\delta^2\eta^q).$$ The supports are intersections of the original supports, or original supports for doubled phases. Since $\delta/\tau$ is small, $$U=U_1+U_2=O_\tau(\delta\tau),\qquad
 dU_1=O_\tau(\delta),\qquad dU_2=O_\tau(\delta^2/\tau).$$ The metric quadratic terms involving $U_2$ are bounded by $$C_m\left(\frac{\delta^3}{\tau}+\frac{\delta^4}{\tau^2}\right)
 \leq C_m\frac{\delta^3}{\tau}.$$ The corresponding terms from $\mathcal R''$ are, by <a href="#eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound">[eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound]</a> at scale $\tau$, bounded by $$C_m\epsilon\tau^{-p}(\delta^3\tau+\delta^4)
 \leq C_m\frac{\delta^3}{\tau}.$$ Here $\epsilon\tau^{-p}\leq\eta\leq1$ and $\delta/\tau\leq1$ suffice.

For completeness, the third-order Taylor estimate is uniform along the whole segment $G+tU$, $0\leq t\leq1$. Indeed $$|j^2U|_{m,\tau}\leq C_m\left(\frac \delta\tau+\frac{\delta^2}{\tau^2}\right),$$ so smallness of $\delta/\tau$ keeps the low jets inside the permitted domain. Also $j^2(G+tU)=O_\tau(1)$, since $\tau\leq s$. The arbitrary-variation version of <a href="#eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound">[eq:conjugated-perturbation-bound]</a> therefore bounds the integral Taylor remainder of $\mathcal R$ by $$C_m\epsilon\tau^{-p}(\delta\tau)^3
 \leq C_m\frac{\delta^3}{\tau}.$$ There is no third-order remainder for $\gamma$ itself.

Finally combine <a href="#eq:free-sum-linear-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-sum-linear-error">[eq:free-sum-linear-error]</a>, <a href="#eq:adjusted-quadratic-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:adjusted-quadratic-mean">[eq:adjusted-quadratic-mean]</a>, and <a href="#eq:quadratic-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-cancellation">[eq:quadratic-cancellation]</a>. Their errors are bounded by $C_m \delta\eta^q$, because $\delta,\tau\leq1$. The remaining terms have just been bounded by $C_m\delta^3/\tau$. This proves <a href="#eq:small-increment-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-increment-estimate">[eq:small-increment-estimate]</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:small-increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:small-increment">6</a>. Every local formula is smooth and differential in cutoff amplitudes, so extension by zero gives the asserted global smooth displacement and support properties. None of the local normal choices requires orientability of $M$.

<a id="stmt-rem-small-polynomial-control"></a>
<div id="rem:small-polynomial-control" class="remark">

*Remark 7* (Dependence on geometric bounds). For $\mathcal P=\gamma$, the constants and inverse smallness thresholds at any fixed accuracy and fixed output order can be bounded polynomially in finitely many input and phase-data bounds and reciprocal nondegeneracy margins. Indeed the coordinate construction uses metric inversion, contractions, division by $|B_{yy}|^2$, and normalized perpendicular products. Derivatives of these expressions have polynomial bounds in their entries, derivatives, and reciprocal denominators. The square roots in <a href="#eq:small-positive-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-positive-decomposition">[eq:small-positive-decomposition]</a> have the same property on a positive input interval. The finite residual corrections and mean substitutions use only a fixed number of these operations. Summing over a varying finite phase collection adds a polynomial dependence on its cardinality. This observation will allow the geometric bounds to depend polynomially on the parameter of an approximate immersion family.

</div>

<a id="sec:exact-correction"></a>
### An exact smooth correction

The small increment of Proposition <a href="#prop:small-increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:small-increment">6</a> leaves a metric error. We now remove that error while keeping the immersion close in $C^2$. Smoothing is used only to construct each increment; the current map itself is never replaced by its smoothing. This preserves its higher derivatives until a sufficiently late stage can control them.

Smoothing in an iteration with derivative loss originates in Nash’s smooth embedding argument (Nash 1956, Part A) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Nash1956](#ref-Nash1956)] and was developed by Moser (Moser 1966, chap. I, §§1 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Moser1966](#ref-Moser1966)]; Hamilton gives a systematic inverse-function formulation (Hamilton 1982, pt. III) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Hamilton1982](#ref-Hamilton1982)]. Here we establish the correction estimates and the passage through the derivative orders directly, without assuming a smooth family of inverses of the full linearized metric map.

<a id="stmt-prop-exact-correction"></a>
<div id="prop:exact-correction" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:exact-input-jets"></a>
<a id="eq:exact-input-second-form"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Exact correction). *Let $M$ be a closed smooth surface, and let $h_*$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on $M$. Fix background norms. There are positive integers $R,N$ with the following property. Suppose that $f_z:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ are smooth immersions for sufficiently small $z>0$, and that <a id="eq:exact-input-metric"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|f_z\|_{C^m}&=O(z^{-m}) &&(0\le m\le R+2),\label{eq:exact-input-jets}\\
 \|\gamma(f_z)-h_*\|_{C^R}&=O(z^N),\label{eq:exact-input-metric}\\
 \min_{p\in M}\max_{|u|=1}|B_{f_z}(u,u)|&\ge c>0.
 \label{eq:exact-input-second-form}
\end{aligned}$$ Then, for all sufficiently small $z$, there is a smooth immersion $\widetilde f_z:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ such that $$\gamma(\widetilde f_z)=h_*,\qquad
 \|\widetilde f_z-f_z\|_{C^2}=o(1).$$ The integers $R,N$ do not depend on the constants in <a href="#eq:exact-input-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-input-jets">[eq:exact-input-jets]</a>–<a href="#eq:exact-input-second-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-input-second-form">[eq:exact-input-second-form]</a>. The threshold on $z$ may depend on them. No regularity of the family with respect to $z$ is required or asserted.*

</div>

The proof has two parts. First we obtain a recurrence valid in every fixed derivative order, from a bound in only one finite order. We then let that controlled order increase, waiting a finite number of steps whenever necessary. The distinction between these two orders is what gives a smooth limit from the finite assumptions above.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Smoothing with a finite input bound

We use the weighted norms introduced in the preceding section. The following elementary smoothing facts also apply to tensor fields.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-order-smoothing"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-order-smoothing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:smooth-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:smooth-approximation"></a>
<a id="eq:smooth-boundedness"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *For each positive integer $r$, there are linear smoothing operators $S_s$, $0<s\le s_0$, on smooth sections of any fixed smooth vector bundle over $M$, with the following properties. For fixed nonnegative integers $q,m$, and $0\le j\le r$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \|S_s u\|_{C^{q+m}}&\le C_{q,m,r}s^{-m}\|u\|_{C^q},
 \label{eq:smooth-derivative}\\
 \|u-S_su\|_{C^{q+j}}&\le C_{q,j,r}s^{r-j}\|u\|_{C^{q+r}},
 \label{eq:smooth-approximation}\\
 \|S_su\|_{C^m}&\le C_{m,r}\|u\|_{C^m}.
 \label{eq:smooth-boundedness}
\end{aligned}$$ The constants use only the fixed atlas, bundle, and displayed orders.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-order-smoothing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-order-smoothing</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a compactly supported smooth kernel $K$ on $\mathbb{R}^2$ with integral one. A finite linear combination of its distinct dilates can be chosen to have integral one and all moments of degrees $1,\ldots,r$ equal to zero. Indeed, if the dilation factors are distinct positive numbers $b_0,\ldots,b_r$, choose their coefficients $c_i$ by $$\sum_{i=0}^r c_i b_i^d=
 \begin{cases}1,&d=0,\\0,&1\le d\le r.\end{cases}$$ The coefficient matrix is a Vandermonde matrix. Every moment of multi-degree $\alpha$ scales by $b_i^{|\alpha|}$, so these equations cancel all the required moments. The resulting kernel need not be positive.

Take a finite coordinate and bundle atlas with a subordinate smooth partition of unity. Extend each localized component by zero in its coordinate chart, convolve at scale $s$, and multiply by a second cutoff equal to one near the original support. For sufficiently small fixed $s_0$, all these operations occur within the charts. Sum the resulting sections. At scale zero this sum is exactly $u$. Taylor’s formula and the moment conditions give <a href="#eq:smooth-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-approximation">[eq:smooth-approximation]</a>. Moving derivatives between the localized component and the kernel gives <a href="#eq:smooth-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-derivative">[eq:smooth-derivative]</a>; moving all derivatives onto the component gives <a href="#eq:smooth-boundedness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-boundedness">[eq:smooth-boundedness]</a>. Derivatives of the fixed cutoffs and bundle frames introduce only lower derivatives with fixed coefficients. This proves all three bounds. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:exact-smoothed-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:exact-smoothing-tail"></a>
We record how these facts will be used. Let $G:M\to\mathbb{R}^4$ be smooth, let $H$ be a smooth symmetric tensor, and define <a id="eq:exact-weighted-input"></a>$$\label{eq:exact-weighted-input}
 \mathcal S_m(t;G,H)=
 \sum_{j=0}^m t^j\bigl(\|G\|_{C^{j+2}}+\|H\|_{C^j}\bigr).$$ Suppose $\mathcal S_r(t;G,H)\le B$, and let $0<\tau\le s\le t\le1$. Set $G_s=S_sG$ and $H_s=S_sH$ using Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-order-smoothing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-order-smoothing">9</a>. Then, for every fixed $m$, $$\begin{aligned}
 |j^2G_s|_{m,s}+|H_s|_{m,s}&\le C_{m,r}B,
 \label{eq:exact-smoothed-bounds}\\
 |j^2(G-G_s)|_{m,\tau}+|H-H_s|_{m,\tau}
 &\le C_{m,r}\left[
 B(s/t)^r+\mathcal S_m(t;G,H)(\tau/t)^r\right].
 \label{eq:exact-smoothing-tail}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first inequality use <a href="#eq:smooth-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-derivative">[eq:smooth-derivative]</a> with $q=2$ and $q=0$, respectively. To prove the second, split the derivatives at $r$. A derivative of order $j\le r$ of $j^2G$ or $H$ contributes at most $$C B t^{-r}s^{r-j}\tau^j
 =C B(s/t)^r(\tau/s)^j
 \le C B(s/t)^r.$$ For $j>r$, boundedness of smoothing in the original derivative norm gives a contribution at most $$C_m(\tau/t)^j\mathcal S_m(t;G,H)
 \le C_m(\tau/t)^r\mathcal S_m(t;G,H).$$ In particular, the constants in <a href="#eq:exact-smoothed-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-smoothed-bounds">[eq:exact-smoothed-bounds]</a> do not use any derivative of the true input above order $r+2$; the possibly large higher derivatives occur only linearly in <a href="#eq:exact-smoothing-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-smoothing-tail">[eq:exact-smoothing-tail]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### A recurrence at every derivative order

<a id="eq:exact-scales"></a>
Fix a small $z$ for the moment. Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-phase-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-phase-data">4</a> provides phase data for $f_z$ with reference metric $h_*$. They remain admissible in the $C^2$ ball of radius $$\rho_z=z^4$$ about $f_z$. Their bounds in every fixed order are polynomial in $z^{-1}$. Put $B=z^{-4}$. The current integer $k\ge10$ controls the amplitude and the number of derivatives to be retained. We use $$\label{eq:exact-scales}
 \beta=\frac65,\qquad \lambda=\frac{11}{10},\qquad
 \tau=t^\beta,\qquad s=t^\lambda,\qquad
 \delta=t^k,\qquad r(k)=q(k)=40(k+1).$$ Here $q(k)$ is the accuracy order in Proposition <a href="#prop:small-increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:small-increment">6</a>. The integer $r(k)$ is the order controlled at the current step; the estimates below will also hold at every fixed observation order $m>r(k)$, without assuming a bound there.

<a id="eq:exact-step-hypotheses"></a>
For a current map $G$, define the normalized defect <a id="eq:exact-normalized-defect"></a>$$\label{eq:exact-normalized-defect}
 H=\delta^{-2}(h_*-\gamma(G)),\qquad
 \mathcal S_m=\mathcal S_m(t;G,H).$$ The step will be applied under the finite-order hypotheses $$\label{eq:exact-step-hypotheses}
 \|G-f_z\|_{C^2}\le\rho_z/2,\qquad
 \|H-h_*\|_{C^0}<\varepsilon_*,\qquad
 \mathcal S_{r(k)}\le B,$$ where $\varepsilon_*>0$ is a fixed sufficiently small radius for the positive tensor decompositions.

<a id="stmt-lem-exact-step"></a>
<div id="lem:exact-step" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:exact-increment-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:exact-all-order-recurrence"></a>
**Lemma 10** (One smoothing step). *Under <a href="#eq:exact-step-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-step-hypotheses">[eq:exact-step-hypotheses]</a>, take either $\delta'=\tau^k$ or $\delta'=\tau^{k+1}$. For sufficiently small $t$, there is a smooth increment $U$ such that, with $$G'=G+U,\qquad H'=\delta'^{-2}(h_*-\gamma(G')),
 \qquad \mathcal S'_m=\mathcal S_m(\tau;G',H'),$$ for every fixed $m\ge0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 |U|_{m,\tau}&\le C_m(z,k)\delta\tau,
 \label{eq:exact-increment-bound}\\
 \mathcal S'_m
 &\le \|G\|_{C^2}+\|h_*\|_{C^0}
       +C_m(z,k)t^{1/5}(1+\mathcal S_m).
 \label{eq:exact-all-order-recurrence}
\end{aligned}$$ The scale threshold and the constants are independent of derivatives of the true inputs $G,H$ above the controlled orders $r(k)+2,r(k)$. For fixed $k$, the inverse threshold is polynomially bounded in $z^{-1}$; for fixed $k,m$, the constants have the same property.*

</div>

The same construction therefore has two different uses. At the controlled order $r(k)$, a small scale preserves its bound. At any higher fixed order, the current norm may be large, but it is multiplied by a coefficient that tends to zero. The latter fact will let us increase $k$ after a finite number of steps.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exact-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exact-step</summary>

*Proof.* Smooth with moment order $r(k)$. By <a href="#eq:exact-smoothing-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-smoothing-tail">[eq:exact-smoothing-tail]</a>, $G_s$ belongs to the admissible $C^2$ ball for small $t$. The tensor $$H_s-(\delta'/\delta)^2h_*$$ belongs to the required neighborhood of $h_*$: the smoothing tail tends to zero, and $\delta'/\delta\to0$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:small-increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:small-increment">6</a> in its unperturbed case to this tensor at $G_s$, with amplitude $\delta$ and accuracy $q(k)$. Write the resulting increment as $U$. The proposition gives <a href="#eq:exact-increment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-increment-bound">[eq:exact-increment-bound]</a> and <a id="eq:exact-mode-error"></a>$$\label{eq:exact-mode-error}
 |E|_{m,\tau}\le C_m(z,k)
 \left[\delta(\tau/s)^{q(k)}+\delta^3/\tau\right],$$ where $$E=\gamma(G_s+U)-\gamma(G_s)
   -\delta^2\bigl[H_s-(\delta'/\delta)^2h_*\bigr].$$ The constants have the stated independence from higher true-input norms because <a href="#eq:exact-smoothed-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-smoothed-bounds">[eq:exact-smoothed-bounds]</a> bounds every required weighted derivative of $G_s,H_s$. For fixed orders they are bounded by fixed powers of $z^{-1}$, by the polynomial phase bounds and the polynomial dependence in Remark <a href="#rem:small-polynomial-control" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rem:small-polynomial-control">7</a>.

<a id="eq:exact-error-identity"></a>
The increment was constructed using $G_s,H_s$, but we add it to the unsmoothed map $G$. To compare the two metric increments, define the symmetric tensor $$\mathcal C(V,W)_{ij}
 =\langle\partial_iV,\partial_jW\rangle
  +\langle\partial_jV,\partial_iW\rangle.$$ Since the induced metric is quadratic in the first derivatives, $$\label{eq:exact-error-identity}
 \delta'^2(H'-h_*)
 =\delta^2(H-H_s)-E-\mathcal C(G-G_s,U).$$ In particular, there is no square of the smoothing tail. From <a href="#eq:exact-increment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-increment-bound">[eq:exact-increment-bound]</a>, $|dU|_{m,\tau}\le C_m(z,k)\delta$; <a href="#eq:exact-smoothing-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-smoothing-tail">[eq:exact-smoothing-tail]</a> bounds the other factor in the last term. Hence the high derivatives of the true input enter the new defect only linearly.

<a id="eq:exact-exponent-table"></a>
For completeness, the powers needed to divide <a href="#eq:exact-error-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-error-identity">[eq:exact-error-identity]</a> by $\delta'^2$ are displayed below. The smaller choice $\delta'=\tau^{k+1}$ is the more demanding one. Using <a href="#eq:exact-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-scales">[eq:exact-scales]</a>, the respective exponents of $t$ are $$\label{eq:exact-exponent-table}
\begin{array}{c|c}
 \text{term}&\text{exponent after division by }\delta'^2\\ \hline
 \delta(\tau/s)^{q(k)}&(13k+8)/5\\
 \delta^3/\tau&(3k-18)/5\\
 \delta^2 B(s/t)^{r(k)}&(18k+8)/5\\
 \delta^2\mathcal S_m(\tau/t)^{r(k)}&(38k+28)/5\\
 \delta B(s/t)^{r(k)}&(13k+8)/5\\
 \delta\mathcal S_m(\tau/t)^{r(k)}&(33k+28)/5.
\end{array}$$ The factors $B$ are included in the constants for fixed $z$. Every exponent is positive for $k\ge10$. We obtain, in particular, <a id="eq:exact-defect-recurrence"></a>$$\label{eq:exact-defect-recurrence}
 |H'-h_*|_{m,\tau}
 \le C_m(z,k)t^{1/5}(1+\mathcal S_m)
 \qquad(m\ge0).$$ This weaker common exponent will also control the map derivatives. Indeed, inherited derivatives of $G$ of positive weighted order gain at least $\tau/t=t^{1/5}$, while $$|j^2U|_{m,\tau}
 \le C_m(z,k)\delta/\tau
 =C_m(z,k)t^{k-6/5}.$$ The fixed tensor satisfies $|h_*|_{m,\tau}\le\|h_*\|_{C^0}+C_m\tau$. Together these estimates give <a href="#eq:exact-all-order-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-all-order-recurrence">[eq:exact-all-order-recurrence]</a>. Its constants use only the smoothed-input bounds, while all higher true-input derivatives have remained in the displayed linear term $\mathcal S_m$. The polynomial bounds already noted for the phase data and finite mode formulas, with the strict positive powers of $t$ in <a href="#eq:exact-exponent-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-exponent-table">[eq:exact-exponent-table]</a>, give the stated polynomial thresholds. ◻

</details>

It remains to initialize this step from the finite assumptions of Proposition <a href="#prop:exact-correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exact-correction">8</a>, keep the maps in the allowed $C^2$ ball, and schedule increases of $k$.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Initialization and passage through the derivative orders

<a id="eq:exact-baseline"></a>
<a id="eq:exact-summable-increment"></a>
Because $\|f_z\|_{C^2}=O(z^{-2})$, for small $z$ the distance condition in <a href="#eq:exact-step-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-step-hypotheses">[eq:exact-step-hypotheses]</a> implies $$\label{eq:exact-baseline}
 \|G\|_{C^2}+\|h_*\|_{C^0}<B/4.$$ For each fixed $z,k$, choose a positive scale threshold that makes Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-step">10</a> applicable, preserves the defect neighborhood, and, by <a href="#eq:exact-all-order-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-all-order-recurrence">[eq:exact-all-order-recurrence]</a> with $m=r(k)$, preserves $\mathcal S_{r(k)}\le B$. Impose also $$\label{eq:exact-summable-increment}
 \|U\|_{C^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}}\le\rho_z t.$$ This last threshold is available because <a href="#eq:exact-increment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-increment-bound">[eq:exact-increment-bound]</a> gives the upper bound $$C(z,k)t^{k+(6/5)(1-\lfloor k/2\rfloor)},$$ and its exponent is greater than one for every $k\ge10$. The thresholds can cover both choices of $\delta'$ at once.

For the initial block $k=10$, only finitely many orders and inequalities have been used. Their constants are polynomial in $z^{-1}$, and every smallness condition has a strict positive power of $t$. Therefore there is a fixed $\mu>1$ such that all these conditions hold for $t\le z^\mu$ and sufficiently small $z$. Enlarge $\mu$ if necessary, and set $$R=r(10)=440,\qquad N>20\mu+1,$$ with $N$ an integer. These choices use polynomial exponents, not the implicit constants in the assumptions of the proposition.

Start at $t=z^\mu$, $k=10$, $\delta=t^{10}$, and $$G=\sqrt{1-\delta^2}\,f_z.$$ Its normalized defect has the exact form $$H=h_*+(1-\delta^{-2})(\gamma(f_z)-h_*).$$ It therefore tends to $h_*$ in $C^R$. The weighted derivatives of $G$ through the required order are bounded by $$C z^{-2}\sum_{j=0}^R z^{(\mu-1)j},$$ so $\mathcal S_R<B/2$ for small $z$. The initial displacement is $O(z^{20\mu-2})$, which is at most $\rho_z/4$ for sufficiently small $z$, since $\mu>1$ and $20\mu-2>4$. This verifies all the initial conditions.

At every subsequent step replace $t$ by $t^{6/5}$. Thus, if $t_n$ is the scale at step $n$, $$t_n=t_0^{(6/5)^n},\qquad \sum_{n=0}^\infty t_n<1/4$$ for small enough $t_0$. Equations <a href="#eq:exact-summable-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-summable-increment">[eq:exact-summable-increment]</a> and the initial displacement keep all maps within $\rho_z/2$ of $f_z$ in $C^2$. Hence the phase data and <a href="#eq:exact-baseline" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-baseline">[eq:exact-baseline]</a> remain valid.

We explain why no derivative order prevents the construction from advancing. Suppose that $k$ is held fixed and let $m$ be any fixed integer. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-step">10</a> repeatedly with $\delta'=\tau^k$. Its all-order recurrence says that the numbers $x_n=\mathcal S_m(t_n;G_n,H_n)$ satisfy $$x_{n+1}\le B/4+c_n(1+x_n),\qquad
 c_n=C_m(z,k)t_n^{1/5}\longrightarrow0.$$ Every $x_n$ is finite because all finite-stage maps are smooth. Once $c_n\le1/4$, this recurrence first bounds the sequence; subsequently $c_n\to0$ gives $\limsup x_n\le B/4$. In particular, after finitely many further steps $x_n\le B$.

Apply this observation with $m=r(k+1)$. Wait in the block with integer $k$ until that higher bound holds and the scale is below the threshold for the block with integer $k+1$. Wait also until $$C_{r(k+1)}(z,k)t^{1/5}(1+B)\le B/2,$$ where the constant is the one for the current block in <a href="#eq:exact-all-order-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-all-order-recurrence">[eq:exact-all-order-recurrence]</a>. All three conditions hold after finitely many steps. Now choose $\delta'=\tau^{k+1}$. That recurrence, already proved for this smaller target amplitude, bounds the new higher norm by $B/4+B/2<B$. The new scale $\tau<t$ also satisfies the next block’s threshold. Thus each block has finite length and $k$ tends to infinity.

For every fixed $m$, all sufficiently late blocks satisfy $\lfloor k/2\rfloor\ge m$. Equation <a href="#eq:exact-summable-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-summable-increment">[eq:exact-summable-increment]</a> then makes the tail of the increments absolutely summable in $C^m$. The maps converge in every $C^m$ to a smooth map $\widetilde f_z$. Their defects tend to zero in $C^0$, since $\delta=t^k\to0$ and $H$ stays in a fixed $C^0$ neighborhood of $h_*$. Continuity of the induced metric in $C^1$ yields $\gamma(\widetilde f_z)=h_*$. Finally, $$\|\widetilde f_z-f_z\|_{C^2}
 \le\rho_z/4+\rho_z\sum_{n=0}^\infty t_n
 \le\rho_z/2\longrightarrow0.$$ This proves Proposition <a href="#prop:exact-correction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exact-correction">8</a>.

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 11*. The finite assumptions control the first block uniformly by powers of $z$. Derivatives above order $R+2$ may be arbitrarily large; at a fixed $z$ they affect only the finite waiting times in later blocks. The construction gives $C^2$ proximity on the whole surface. It does not assert that the exact correction vanishes outside a prescribed support.

</div>

<a id="sec:velocity-loop"></a>
### A velocity circle with controlled turns

We now construct the leading oscillation for Proposition <a href="#prop:primitive-addition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:primitive-addition">5</a>. Throughout this section its data $F,D,a,x,y,n$ and the later curves are fixed. The basic choice is to oscillate in the plane perpendicular to both $G_y$ and $G_{yy}$, for a slow map $G$ near $F$. Orthogonality to the second derivative will remove the first error in the $yy$ metric coefficient. A separate choice at the two turning points of the velocity path will preserve the later boundary conditions. For a $2\pi$-periodic function $b$ we write $\operatorname{avg}b=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_0^{2\pi}b(\theta)\,d\theta$. For a nonzero vector $b$, write $\widehat b=b/|b|$.

The use of a periodic velocity to add a rank-one metric tensor belongs to the oscillatory constructions of Nash and Kuiper (Nash 1954; Kuiper 1955) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Nash1954](#ref-Nash1954), [Kuiper1955](#ref-Kuiper1955)]; see also (Choquet 1958, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Choquet1958](#ref-Choquet1958)] and (Misha Gromov 2015, secs. 3.2–3.3) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Gromov2015](#ref-Gromov2015)]. The second-derivative constraint on the velocity plane and the behavior prescribed at its turns are part of the construction proved here.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The plane and the leading metric

<a id="eq:velocity-plane"></a>
For a 2-jet near that of $F$, define $$Y=G_y,\qquad C=G_{yy},\qquad
 P=\operatorname{span}(Y,C)^\perp,\qquad
 v=PG_x,\qquad R_0=(|v|^2+a^2)^{1/2}.
 \label{eq:velocity-plane}$$ We use $P$ also for orthogonal projection to this plane. The following facts justify these definitions and the frame we will use.

<a id="stmt-lem-velocity-plane"></a>
<div id="lem:velocity-plane" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *On a neighborhood of $\overline D$ and a sufficiently small neighborhood of the 2-jet section of $F$, the vectors $Y,C$ are independent, $P$ is a smooth 2-plane, and $R_0$ is bounded below by a positive constant. Projection to $P$ restricts to an isomorphism on the normal plane of $G$. There is a smooth orthonormal frame $(e_1,e_2)$ of $P$, depending smoothly on these jets, such that $e_1=v/|v|$ on a collar of $C_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-velocity-plane"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-velocity-plane</summary>

<a id="eq:projected-yy-form"></a>
*Proof.* In the coordinates $(x,y)$, $$\operatorname{Hess}_{\gamma(G)}x(\partial_y,\partial_y)=-\Gamma^x_{yy},\qquad
 C=\Gamma^x_{yy}G_x+\Gamma^y_{yy}G_y+B_G(\partial_y,\partial_y).$$ By <a href="#eq:primitive-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primitive-hessian">[eq:primitive-hessian]</a>, $\Gamma^x_{yy}<0$ at $F$ on $\overline D$, and this inequality persists on the indicated neighborhoods. The tangential projections of $Y,C$ are therefore independent. If a normal vector belongs to $\operatorname{span}(Y,C)$, its zero tangential projection forces both coefficients to vanish. Projection from the normal plane to $P$ is consequently an isomorphism. Projecting the displayed decomposition of $C$ gives $$P B_G(\partial_y,\partial_y)=-\Gamma^x_{yy}v.
 \label{eq:projected-yy-form}$$ At $F$, $A_0\ne0$ on $C_0$, so $v\ne0$ on a collar. Moreover $v$ and $Pn$ are not opposite rays there, by <a href="#eq:boundary-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-invariants">[eq:boundary-invariants]</a>, <a href="#eq:projected-yy-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-yy-form">[eq:projected-yy-form]</a>, and the injectivity of projection on the old normal plane.

The vector $Pn$ is nonzero over the whole disk at $F$, and remains so for nearby jets. On a smaller collar set $e_1=\widehat v$, and in the interior set $e_1=\widehat{Pn}$. In the intervening collar, normalize a smooth convex interpolation of these two non-antipodal vectors. Orient $P$ using the ordered pair $(Y,C)$ and the orientation of $\mathbb{R}^4$, and complete $e_1$ to $e_2$ with that orientation. These choices are local to this coordinate disk.

Finally $R_0>0$ because $a>0$ in $D$ and $v\ne0$ near its boundary. Compactness gives a positive lower bound, which persists after shrinking the jet neighborhood and the exterior neighborhood. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:leading-velocity"></a>
For an angular function $\alpha$ to be chosen, set $$p=\cos\alpha\,e_1+\sin\alpha\,e_2,\qquad
 V=R_0p,\qquad w=V-v,\qquad X=G_x+w.
 \label{eq:leading-velocity}$$ Since $G_x-v\in\operatorname{span}(Y,C)$ and $V,v\in P$, <a id="eq:leading-metric"></a>$$|X|^2=|G_x|^2+a^2,\qquad X\cdot Y=G_x\cdot G_y.
 \label{eq:leading-metric}$$ Thus the pair $(X,Y)$ has exactly the required leading metric. What remains is to make the periodic mean of $w$ zero, while controlling the places where the velocity stops along its circle.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### A prescribed mean and smooth vanishing

Initially use an angular parameter $t\in\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb Z)$ and angles <a id="eq:unreparametrized-angle"></a>$$\alpha=h_{\mathrm{turn}}+A\cos t.
 \label{eq:unreparametrized-angle}$$ Here $h_{\mathrm{turn}}$ will be a smooth function supported in a fixed compact subset of $D$ and small in value. We first choose $A$, independently of that function.

<a id="eq:small-arc-mean"></a>
<a id="eq:smooth-small-amplitude"></a>
On a sufficiently small collar, $e_1=\widehat v$ and $a/|v|$ is small. There is a nonnegative smooth amplitude $A_{\mathrm{sm}}$ satisfying $$\frac1{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}\cos(A_{\mathrm{sm}}\cos t)\,dt
 =\frac{|v|}{R_0}.
 \label{eq:small-arc-mean}$$ Indeed the left side is $1-A_{\mathrm{sm}}^2/4+O(A_{\mathrm{sm}}^4)$. The implicit function theorem applied to the squared amplitude gives $$A_{\mathrm{sm}}=\frac a{|v|}\,
 c\left(\frac{a^2}{|v|^2}\right),\qquad c(0)=\sqrt2,
 \label{eq:smooth-small-amplitude}$$ where $c$ is positive and smooth. Thus $A_{\mathrm{sm}}$ is smooth and flat on $C_0$, and it is zero on the exterior. When $h_{\mathrm{turn}}=0$, the sine average vanishes as well, so the unweighted mean of $R_0p$ is exactly $v$ on this collar.

Farther inward, while still in a region where $e_1=\widehat v$ and the small formula is defined, interpolate $A$ from $A_{\mathrm{sm}}$ to a fixed number larger than $\pi$, keeping $A\ge A_{\mathrm{sm}}$. Use that constant on the remaining interior. For $a>0$ the point $v/R_0$ lies strictly inside the convex hull of the path $p$ when $h_{\mathrm{turn}}=0$. On the small arc this follows because its unweighted mean lies strictly between the endpoint chord and the point $(1,0)$ in the $(e_1,e_2)$ coordinates. Increasing $A$ retains this arc; when $A>\pi$ the path covers the circle and $|v|/R_0<1$. On any fixed compact subset of the interior, this strict membership persists for sufficiently small $\|h_{\mathrm{turn}}\|_{C^0}$, uniformly on a compact neighborhood of the permitted jet data.

We use the following elementary fact to adjust the mean.

<a id="stmt-lem-loop-density"></a>
<div id="lem:loop-density" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Smooth densities for interior averages). *Let a smooth family of circle-valued paths $p_b(t)\in\mathbb{R}^2$ and targets $c_b\in\mathbb{R}^2$ be parametrized by a finite-dimensional parameter $b$. On a compact parameter set, suppose each $c_b$ lies in the interior of the convex hull of $p_b$. Then, on a neighborhood of that set, there are smoothly varying strictly positive densities $\rho_b(t)$ such that $$\operatorname{avg}_t\rho_b=1,\qquad \operatorname{avg}_t(\rho_b p_b)=c_b.$$ These densities may be patched with any already prescribed positive solutions on an overlapping parameter neighborhood.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-loop-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-loop-density</summary>

*Proof.* At a fixed parameter choose finitely many path values whose convex hull contains the target in its interior. Choose strictly positive weights with this barycenter and total mass one. Replacing the point masses by sufficiently narrow smooth bumps, and adding a sufficiently small positive constant density, preserves the interior condition. The three linear constraints, for mass and the two coordinates, have rank three. A fixed right inverse of their coefficient matrix gives nearby positive weights solving the constraints smoothly as the parameter varies. A finite parameter cover and a smooth partition of unity patch these local solutions: the constraints are linear in the density and positivity is preserved by convex combinations. The same argument patches with an already prescribed solution. ◻

</details>

Apply the lemma in the frame $(e_1,e_2)$, with $c=v/R_0$, and use $\rho=1$ on a smaller collar where <a href="#eq:small-arc-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-arc-mean">[eq:small-arc-mean]</a> applies and $h_{\mathrm{turn}}=0$. Here the parameters are position and the $2$-jet of $G$. The frame identifies all the velocity planes with $\mathbb{R}^2$, and the remaining parameter set is compact and lies in $a>0$. The interior hypothesis therefore has the required uniform margin, and the lemma patches a smooth density with the prescribed collar solution. Put $$\theta=\int_0^t\rho(s)\,ds$$ and replace $t$ by the smooth inverse of this orientation-preserving circle diffeomorphism. We henceforth use $\theta$ as periodic parameter. Its uniform mean satisfies <a id="eq:velocity-mean"></a>$$\operatorname{avg}_\theta V=v,\qquad \operatorname{avg}_\theta w=0.
 \label{eq:velocity-mean}$$ All data are smooth functions of position and the 2-jet of $G$. On the exterior $A=0$, $h_{\mathrm{turn}}=0$, and $\rho=1$, so $V=v$. Equations <a href="#eq:smooth-small-amplitude" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-small-amplitude">[eq:smooth-small-amplitude]</a> and <a href="#eq:small-arc-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-arc-mean">[eq:small-arc-mean]</a> show that $w$ is smooth across $C_0$ and vanishes there with every spatial derivative.

<a id="eq:turn-derivative"></a>
In the interior $A>0$. There are exactly two zeros of $\alpha_\theta$, corresponding to $t=0,\pi$. At these turns, $$\alpha=h_{\mathrm{turn}}\pm A,
 \qquad \alpha_y=(h_{\mathrm{turn}})_y\pm A_y.
 \label{eq:turn-derivative}$$ In this formula spatial differentiation holds the new parameter $\theta$ fixed. The additional chain-rule term from the inverse reparametrization is zero because it is multiplied by $\sin t$. Figure <a href="#fig:velocity-collar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:velocity-collar">1</a> separates the velocity circle from its angular parametrization. The two stationary angles persist under the positive reparametrization; their transverse derivatives are the quantities we will prescribe along the later curves.

<figure id="fig:velocity-collar">

<figcaption>Velocity circle, its angular parametrization, and the support collar. The circle in $P = span (Y,C)^{⊥}$ has radius $R_{0} = (|v|^{2}+a^{2})^{1/2}$; $V − v$ is the correction vector. The middle panel plots $α = h_{turn} + Acos t$ before the positive reparametrization that gives mean velocity $v$. It has two turns per period, at $t = 0 ≡ 2π$ and $t = π$. There $α_{y} = (h_{turn})_{y} ± A_{y}$, even after reparametrization, so a large transverse derivative can control both turns. The angle graph also applies when $A > π$ and the velocity path wraps around the circle. As $a$ vanishes at $C_{0}$, the path contracts smoothly to $v$, while the circle retains radius $|v| > 0$. The exact immersions approach the old map in $C^{2}$ outside $D$ as the fast scale tends to zero.</figcaption>
</figure>

Three diagram panels arranged horizontally:
1. 2. Middle panel ("Angle before reparametrization"): A Cartesian plot with horizontal axis $t$ marked at $0$, $\pi$, and $2\pi$, and vertical axis $\alpha$ with an upward arrow. Three horizontal dashed reference lines at $\alpha = h_{\mathrm{turn}} + A$, $\alpha = h_{\mathrm{turn}}$, and $\alpha = h_{\mathrm{turn}} - A$. A cosine curve starts at $(0, h_{\mathrm{turn}} + A)$, reaches a minimum at $(\pi, h_{\mathrm{turn}} - A)$, and returns to a maximum at $(2\pi, h_{\mathrm{turn}} + A)$. Arrows point to the crest at $t = 0$ and the trough at $t = \pi$, each labeled "turn".
3. Right panel ("Support collar"): A shaded disk labeled $D$ bounded by an inner circle, surrounded by a concentric outer boundary labeled $C_0$. The annular region between the inner disk and $C_0$ is pointed to by an arrow labeled "collar".
